Then, taking off his kamilavkion, he exclaims, in place of "Great is the name of the Holy Trinity": "Christ is risen!" once. And as we answer: "Truly He is risen!" The same, having signed the Artos crosswise, says: "We worship His third-day Resurrection." He places it upon the panagiarion. Then the cellarer brings the Artos with the panagiarion unto the Superior, and kisses the Artos; and all the brethren after him, singing Ode 9. Heirmos: "Shine, shine, O New Jerusalem..."; and the other two troparia of that ode; and again the Heirmos: "Shine, shine, O New Jerusalem..." And after the kissing of the prosphora by all, the cellarer, having returned, sets it before the Superior, and recites the verse: "Through the prayers of our holy fathers, O Lord Jesus Christ our God, have mercy upon us." And we: "Amen." And we sing: "Christ is risen from the dead..." thrice. Glory: Hypakoe: "When they that were with Mary came before the dawn..." Both now: Kontakion: "Though Thou didst descend into the grave..." "Lord, have mercy" thrice. "Bless, master." Superior: "Blessed is God Who is merciful, and Who feeds us from His rich gifts, by His grace and love for mankind, always, both now and for ever and unto ages of ages." And we: "Amen." Then the Superior: "May God forgive and have mercy upon those who have served us." And immediately he goes forth from the refectory unto the church, and all the brethren according to their rank, the Priest going before with the icon of the Resurrection of Christ, and the Deacon with the Artos, and the clergy singing: "Christ is risen from the dead..." Then the paraecclesiarch and the other brethren strike all the bells. And when the Superior and the brethren have entered into the church, the Priest and the Deacon place the icon and the Artos in their places; and we sing: "And unto us hath He granted eternal life..." And we go unto our cells. In this manner the veneration is performed over the prosphora which is the Artos throughout all the week of the holy and bright Sunday, even unto Saturday.*

*It is fitting to know: That if it happen that any brother depart unto the Lord during this holy week of Pascha, concerning this see the entire order of these days for the departed in the Trebnik.*[^414]

## On the Holy and Great Sunday of Pascha, at Vespers[^415]

*After the Ninth Hour, at the Lucernarium [lamp-lighting time], the Superior vests in all his priestly vestments. And standing before the holy table with the censer, he signs crosswise, and exclaims, saying:*

**Priest:** Blessed is our God, always, both now and for ever and unto ages of ages.

**Choir:** Amen.

*And he begins:*

Christ is risen from the dead, trampling down death by death, and upon those in the tombs bestowing life. *(thrice)*

*And we respond the same thrice. And the Superior recites the aforesaid verses:*

*Verse 1: Let God arise, and let His enemies be scattered, and let them that hate Him flee from before His face! (Ps. 67:2 [68:1])*

*Verse 2: As smoke vanishes, so let them vanish: as wax melts before the face of fire! (Ps. 67:3 [68:2])*

*Verse 3: So let the sinners perish from the face of God, but let the righteous be glad! (Ps. 67:3b-4a [68:2b-3a])*

*Verse 4: This is the day which the Lord hath made: let us rejoice and be glad therein! (Ps. 117:24 [118:24])*

*And after each verse we sing: "Christ is risen from the dead..." thrice. Glory: we sing: "Christ is risen from the dead..." Both now: we sing: "Christ is risen from the dead..." And again the Superior:*

Christ is risen from the dead, trampling down death by death:

*And we the conclusion:*

and upon those in the tombs bestowing life.

*Then the Great Litany by the Deacon:*

**Deacon:** In peace let us pray to the Lord.

*It is fitting to know: That after the Priest begins, he censes the holy table according to custom and the entire sanctuary; at Vespers, at "Lord, I have cried."*

*At Matins, at the beginning of the canon, he goes forth and censes the temple, the choirs, and the brethren according to the customary order.*

*Thereafter: "Lord, I have cried." We sing in Tone 2, setting 6 verses, and we sing 3 resurrectional stichera:*[^416]

*Tone 2:*

Come, let us worship God the Word, begotten of the Father before the ages, incarnate of the Virgin Mary; for having endured the Cross, He surrendered Himself unto burial as He Himself willed, and having risen from the dead, He saved me, the wandering man.

*Sticheron:*

Christ our Savior, having nailed unto the Cross the handwriting that was against us, wiped it out, and abolished the dominion of death: we worship His third-day Resurrection.

*Sticheron:*

With the Archangels let us sing the Resurrection of Christ; for He is the Redeemer and Savior of our souls: and in fearful glory and mighty power He comes again to judge the world which He hath created.

*And 3 Anatolian [Eastern] Stichera, Tone 2:*[^417]

*Sticheron:*

The angel proclaimed Thee as Master, Who wast crucified and buried, and he said unto the women: Come, see where the Lord lay! For He is risen as He said, being almighty; therefore do we worship Thee, the only Immortal One: O Christ, Giver of life, have mercy upon us!

*Sticheron:*

By Thy Cross Thou didst abolish the curse that came from the tree; by Thy burial Thou didst put to death the dominion of death; and by Thine arising Thou didst enlighten the human race: therefore do we cry aloud unto Thee: O Christ our God, Benefactor, glory to Thee!

*Sticheron:*

The gates of death opened unto Thee in fear, O Lord, and the gatekeepers of hades, beholding Thee, were afraid; for Thou didst shatter the brazen gates and didst crush the iron bars, and Thou didst lead us forth from darkness and the shadow of death, and didst break our bonds asunder!

*Glory:*

Singing a song of salvation, let us send up praise from our mouths: Come, let us all fall down in the house of the Lord, saying: O Thou Who wast crucified upon the Tree, and art risen from the dead, and art in the bosom of the Father, cleanse our sins!

*Both now: Theotokion (Dogmatikon), Tone 2:*[^418]

The shadow of the law passed away when grace arrived; for as the bush burned yet was not consumed, so as a Virgin Thou didst give birth, and a Virgin didst remain: in place of the pillar of fire, the Sun of Righteousness shone forth; in place of Moses, Christ, the Salvation of our souls.

*Entrance with the Gospel.*[^419]

*O Gladsome Light.*

*Great Prokeimenon, Tone 7:*[^420]

What God is so great as our God? Thou art the God Who workest wonders! (Ps. 76:14-15a [77:13-14a])

*Verse 1: Thou hast made known Thy power among the peoples! (Ps. 76:15b [77:14b]) Verse 2: And I said: Now have I begun; this is the change of the right hand of the Most High! (Ps. 76:11 [77:10])*

*Verse 3: I remembered the works of the Lord, for I will remember Thy wonders from the beginning! (Ps. 76:12 [77:11])*

*And again: "What God is so great as our God? Thou art the God Who workest wonders!"*

*After the Prokeimenon the Deacon exclaims:*

**Deacon:** And that we may be accounted worthy to hear the Holy Gospel, let us pray unto the Lord God.

*After this the Superior reads the Gospel in the sanctuary, John, pericope 65 (John 20:19-25):*[^421]

When it was evening on that day, the first day of the week, and the doors were shut where the disciples were assembled for fear of the Jews, Jesus came and stood in the midst, and saith unto them: Peace be unto you. And having said this, He showed unto them His hands and His feet and His side. Then were the disciples glad when they saw the Lord. Then said Jesus unto them again: Peace be unto you: as the Father hath sent Me, even so send I you. And when He had said this, He breathed on them, and saith unto them: Receive ye the Holy Spirit: whosesoever sins ye forgive, they are forgiven unto them; and whosesoever sins ye retain, they are retained. But Thomas, one of the twelve, called Didymus [the Twin], was not with them when Jesus came. The other disciples therefore said unto him: We have seen the Lord. But he said unto them: Except I shall see in His hands the print of the nails, and put my finger into the print of the nails, and thrust my hand into His side, I will not believe.

*After this, the Litany: "Let us all say..."; and "Vouchsafe, O Lord, to keep us this evening..."; Then: "Let us complete our evening prayer..."; and after the exclamation we sing at the Aposticha, the resurrectional sticheron, Tone 2:*

Thy Resurrection, O Christ Savior, hath enlightened all the universe, and Thou hast called back Thine own creation: almighty Lord, glory to Thee!

*Then the Paschal Stichera, Tone 5: "A sacred Pascha...", with their verses. Glory, both now: "It is the day of Resurrection..."; "Christ is risen from the dead..." thrice.*

*Then the Deacon:*

**Deacon:** Wisdom!

*Choir:*

**Choir:** Bless!

*Superior:*

**Priest:** Blessed is Christ our God, Who is...

*And we:*

**Choir:** Confirm, O God...

*Then the Superior, in place of "Glory to Thee, O Christ God":*

**Priest:** Christ is risen from the dead!

*And we:*

**Choir:** and upon those in the tombs bestowing life.

*And immediately he pronounces the dismissal with the Cross: "Christ is risen from the dead...", and the rest as indicated at the end of Matins. And we venerate the holy icons.*

*And we enter into the refectory, and say: "Christ is risen from the dead..." thrice. Glory, both now. Superior: "O Christ God, bless..." Upon rising from table, we say: "Christ is risen from the dead..." thrice. Glory, both now. Superior: "God is with us by His grace..."*

*And thus do we perform the order of Vespers throughout all Bright Week.*

## On Monday of Bright Week, at Matins[^422]

*Before the beginning, the paraecclesiarch lights the lamps and candles. Then he strikes the great bell, and the rest. And when we have assembled in the temple, the Priest makes the customary bow to the Superior, and goes into the holy sanctuary, and taking the censer stands before the holy table, without his priestly vestments, having put on only the epitrachelion over his mantle: he censes crosswise, saying:*

**Priest:** Glory to the holy, consubstantial, and life-creating...

*And he begins the Troparion:*

Christ is risen from the dead... *(thrice)*

*And we respond the same.*

*And we sing: "Christ is risen from the dead..." with its verses, as was indicated for Sunday at Matins and Vespers. But in parish churches the Priest vests in his vestments. Then the Great Litany. And after the exclamation we immediately sing the Canon. Heirmos: "It is the day of Resurrection..." twice, and the troparia to 6, with the refrain: "Christ is risen from the dead." Katavasia by both choirs together: "It is the day of Resurrection..." After Ode 3, the Hypakoe: "When they that were with Mary came before the dawn..." And the reading from the First Book: "In the beginning was the Word..." (from John, the commentary of Saint John Chrysostom; for from this day we begin this book, and complete it even unto All Saints). After Ode 6, the Kontakion: "Though Thou didst descend into the grave...", and the Ikos, and reading. And after it: "Having beheld the Resurrection of Christ..." thrice, and "Jesus is risen from the tomb..." thrice. And immediately we sing the remaining odes: the 7th, 8th, and 9th odes.*[^423]

*Then the Exapostilarion: "Having fallen asleep in the flesh..." thrice.*

*At the Praises: "Let every breath..."*

*And we sing the resurrectional stichera, Tone 2:*

Let every breath and all creation glorify Thee, O Lord, for by the Cross Thou didst abolish death, that Thou mightest show unto the people Thy Resurrection from the dead, as the only Lover of mankind.

*Sticheron:*

Let the Jews say how the soldiers lost the King Whom they guarded! For why did not the stone preserve the Rock of life? Either let them produce Him that was buried, or let them worship Him that is risen, saying with us: Glory to the multitude of Thy compassions, O our Savior, glory to Thee!

*Sticheron:*

Rejoice and be glad, O ye people! An angel sitting upon the stone of the tomb, he brought us the glad tidings, saying: Christ is risen from the dead, the Savior of the world, and hath filled all things with sweet fragrance: rejoice and be glad, O ye people!

*Sticheron:*

An angel indeed brought the greeting "Rejoice!" unto Her that is full of grace before Thy Conception, O Lord; and an angel also rolled away the stone of Thy glorious tomb at Thy Resurrection: the former indeed announcing tokens of gladness in place of sorrow; and the latter proclaiming unto us the Master, the Giver of life, in place of death: therefore do we cry aloud unto Thee: O Lord, Benefactor of all, glory to Thee!

*And the Paschal Stichera with their verses, as was prescribed for Sunday at Matins. And the litanies, and dismissal.*

*And the First Hour.*

*And in this manner the service of Matins is conducted throughout all Bright Week. We also make a Litiya outside the monastery.*[^424]

*It is fitting to know: That we make a procession today outside the monastery, bearing banners before us. Holding in our hands the Holy Gospel, the precious Cross, and the icons of the Resurrection of Christ and of the Most Pure Mother of God. We sing the Paschal Canon with the Theotokia to 8. And for Katavasia, the same Heirmos.*

*After Ode 6, following the Gospel, we say: "Having beheld the Resurrection of Christ..." thrice. Then the Sticheron: "Jesus is risen from the tomb..." thrice.*

*Then Odes 7, 8, and 9. And the remainder of the Moleben.*

*At the Divine Liturgy, the Paschal Antiphons are sung without change, as indicated on the Sunday of Pascha.*

*Prokeimenon of the Epistle, Tone 8:*

Their sound hath gone forth into all the earth, and their words unto the ends of the universe! (Ps. 18:5 [19:4])

*Verse: The heavens declare the glory of God, and the firmament proclaims the work of His hands! (Ps. 18:2 [19:1])*

*The Reading from the Acts of the Apostles, pericope 2 (Acts 1:12-17, 21-26):*[^425]

In those days, the Apostles returned unto Jerusalem...

*Alleluia, Tone 1:*

*Verse: The heavens shall confess Thy wonders, O Lord! (Ps. 88:6a [89:5a])*

*Verse: God is glorified in the council of the saints! (Ps. 88:8a [89:7a])*

*The Reading from the Holy Gospel according to John, pericope 2 (John 1:18-28):*[^426]

No man hath seen God at any time...

*Kinonikon [Communion Hymn]:*

Receive ye the Body of Christ...

*At the refectory there is great consolation for the brethren.*

## On Monday of Bright Week, at Vespers[^427]

*At the Lucernarium, after "Christ is risen from the dead" and the rest,*

*"Lord, I have cried": in Tone 3. We set 6 verses, and we sing the resurrectional stichera:*

*Tone 3:*

By Thy Cross, O Christ Savior, the dominion of death hath been destroyed, and the deception of the devil abolished: and the human race, saved by faith, ever offers a hymn unto Thee.

*Sticheron:*

All things have been enlightened by Thy Resurrection, O Lord, and paradise is opened again: and all creation, praising Thee, ever offers a hymn unto Thee.

*Sticheron:*

I glorify the power of the Father and the Son, and I praise the authority of the Holy Spirit: undivided, uncreated Godhead, consubstantial Trinity, reigning unto the age of the age.

*And 3 Anatolian [Eastern] Stichera, in the same Tone:*

*Sticheron:*

We venerate Thy precious Cross, O Christ, and Thy Resurrection we sing and glorify; for by Thy wound were we all healed.

*Sticheron:*

We hymn the Savior incarnate of the Virgin; for for our sake was He crucified, and on the third day He arose, granting us great mercy.

*Sticheron:*

Descending unto those in hades, Christ brought the glad tidings, saying: Be of good cheer, now have I conquered! I am the Resurrection, I will lead you forth, having destroyed the gates of death!

*Glory:*

Standing unworthily in Thy most pure house, we send up an evening hymn, crying out from the depth, O Christ God: O Thou Who didst enlighten the world by Thy third-day Resurrection, deliver Thy people from the hand of Thine enemies, O Lover of mankind!

*Both now: Theotokion (Dogmatikon), Tone 3:*[^428]

How can we not marvel at Thy divine-human childbearing, O Most Pure Lady? For without having accepted the trial of a man, O All-Blameless One, Thou didst give birth in the flesh without father unto a Son: Who was begotten of the Father without mother before the ages, having undergone no change, nor mingling, nor division; but preserving intact the specific property of each of the two essences. Therefore, O Mother and Virgin Lady, beseech Him to save the souls of those who in orthodox fashion confess Thee to be the Mother of God.

*Entrance with the censer.*

*O Gladsome Light.*

*Prokeimenon, Tone 7:*

Our God is in heaven and on earth: whatsoever He hath willed, He hath done! (Ps. 113:11 [115:3])[^429]

*Verse 1: At the going forth of Israel out of Egypt, of the house of Jacob from a barbarous people, Judea became His sanctuary! (Ps. 113:1-2a [114:1-2a])*

*Verse 2: The sea beheld it and fled: the Jordan was turned back! (Ps. 113:3 [114:3])*

*Verse 3: What ails thee, O sea, that thou fleddest; and thou, O Jordan, that thou wast turned back? (Ps. 113:5 [114:5])*

*And again:*

Our God is in heaven and on earth: whatsoever He hath willed, He hath done!

*Priest: "Let us all say..."; and the rest of the litanies. "Vouchsafe, O Lord..."; Then: "Let us complete our evening prayer..."; and after the exclamation:*

*At the Aposticha, the resurrectional sticheron, Tone 3:*

O Christ, Who by Thy Passion didst darken the sun, and by the light of Thy Resurrection didst enlighten all things: accept our evening hymn, O Lover of mankind!

*And the Paschal Stichera with their verses: "Christ is risen from the dead..." thrice.*

*And the dismissal.*

## On Tuesday of Bright Week, at Matins[^430]

*The entire order is conducted without change, as was prescribed above.*

*At the Praises, we set 4 verses, and we sing the resurrectional stichera:*

*Tone 3:*

Come, all ye nations, understand the power of the awesome mystery: for Christ our Savior, the Word Who was in the beginning, was crucified for our sake, and voluntarily was buried, and arose from the dead, that He might save all things: unto Him let us bow down!

*Sticheron:*

Thy guards proclaimed all Thy wonders, O Lord: but the council of vanity filled their right hand with bribes, thinking to conceal Thy Resurrection, which the world glorifies: have mercy upon us!

All things were filled with joy, having received the proof of the Resurrection: for Mary Magdalene came unto the tomb, and found an Angel sitting upon the stone, gleaming in raiment, and saying: Why seek ye the Living among the dead? He is not here, but is risen, as He said, going before you into Galilee.

In Thy light, O Master, we shall see light, O Lover of mankind: for Thou art risen from the dead, granting salvation unto the human race, that all creation may glorify Thee, the only Sinless One: have mercy upon us!

*And the stichera of the Feast: "A sacred Pascha...", with their verses.*

*Then: "Christ is risen from the dead...", thrice. The litanies, and the dismissal. And the First Hour.*

*At the Liturgy, the Antiphons of Pascha.*

*Prokeimenon of the Apostle, Tone 3. The Song of the Mother of God:*
*My soul doth magnify the Lord, and my spirit hath rejoiced in God my Savior. (Luke 1:46b-47)*
*Verse: For He hath regarded the lowliness of His handmaiden: for behold, from henceforth all generations shall call me blessed. (Luke 1:48)*

*The Reading from the Acts of the Apostles, pericope 4 (Acts 2:14-21):*[^431]
*Peter, standing up...*

*Alleluia, Tone 8:*
*Arise, O Lord, into Thy rest: Thou, and the ark of Thy sanctuary! (Ps. 131:8)*
*Verse: The Lord hath sworn the truth unto David, and He will not turn away from it. (Ps. 131:11a)*

*The Reading from the Holy Gospel according to Luke, pericope 113 (Luke 24:12-35):*
*Peter arising, ran unto the tomb...*

*Kinonikon [Communion Hymn]:*
*Receive ye the Body of Christ; taste ye of the immortal Fountain.*[^432]

## On Tuesday of Bright Week, at Vespers[^433]

*At "Lord, I have cried", we set 6 verses, and we sing the resurrectional stichera, Tone 4:*

*Tone 4:*

Ever worshipping Thy life-creating Cross, O Christ God, we glorify Thy third-day Resurrection: for thereby hast Thou renewed corrupted human nature, O Almighty One, and hast renewed for us the ascent unto the heavens, as the only good One and Lover of mankind.

Thou didst loose the penalty of the tree of disobedience, O Savior, by willingly being nailed unto the tree of the Cross; and descending into hades, O Mighty One, Thou didst tear asunder the bonds of death as God: therefore we bow down before Thy Resurrection from the dead, crying out with joy: O Almighty Lord, glory to Thee!

Thou hast shattered the gates of hades, O Lord, and by Thy Death hast Thou dissolved the kingdom of death; and Thou hast freed the human race from corruption, granting life and incorruption unto the world, and great mercy.

*And 3 Anatolian [Eastern] Stichera, in the same Tone:*

*Sticheron:*

Come, ye peoples, let us hymn the third-day Rising of the Savior, whereby we have been delivered from the unbreakable bonds of hades, and all of us have received incorruption and life, crying out: O Thou Who wast crucified, and buried, and didst rise: save us by Thy Resurrection, O only Lover of mankind!

Angels and men, O Savior, hymn Thy third-day Rising, whereby the ends of the universe have been illuminated, and all of us have been delivered from the bondage of the enemy, crying out: O Life-Creator, Almighty Savior, save us by Thy Resurrection, O only Lover of mankind!

Thou hast crushed the gates of brass, and hast shattered the bars, O Christ God, and hast raised up fallen human nature: therefore with one accord we cry aloud: O Lord Who didst rise from the dead, glory to Thee!

*Glory:*

O Lord, Thy Nativity from the Father is timeless and eternal; Thy Incarnation from the Virgin is ineffable and unutterable unto men; and Thy descent into hades is terrible unto the devil and his angels: for having trampled down death, Thou didst rise on the third day, bestowing incorruption and great mercy upon men.

*Both now: Theotokion (Dogmatikon), Tone 4:*[^434]

David the prophet, who through thee became the forefather of God, announced thee in song concerning the great things done for thee by Him: The Queen stood at Thy right hand; for God, Who willed to become man without a father from thee, revealed thee as Mother and mediatrix of life, that He might renew His own image corrupted by passions, and having found the sheep that went astray on the mountain, He might take it upon His shoulders and bring it unto the Father, and according to His will unite it with the heavenly hosts, and Christ, Who hath great and rich mercy, might save the world, O Mother of God.

*Entrance with the censer.*

*O Gladsome Light.*

*Prokeimenon, Tone 8:*

With my voice have I cried unto the Lord, with my voice unto God, and He gave ear unto me! (Ps. 76:2 [77:1])

*Verse 1: In the day of my trouble I sought God. (Ps. 76:3a [77:2a])*

*Verse 2: My soul refused to be comforted. (Ps. 76:3b [77:2b])*

*Verse 3: O God, in the sanctuary is Thy way! (Ps. 76:14a [77:13a])*

*And again:*

With my voice have I cried unto the Lord, with my voice unto God, and He gave ear unto me!

*At the Aposticha, the resurrectional sticheron, Tone 4:*

O Lord, ascending upon the Cross, Thou didst abolish our ancestral curse; and descending into hades, Thou didst free those who were bound from the ages, granting incorruption unto the human race: therefore singing we glorify Thy life-creating and saving Rising.

*Then the Paschal Stichera, with their verses.*

*And the rest as was prescribed, and the dismissal.*

## On Wednesday of Bright Week, at Matins[^435]

*The entire order of the Feast is conducted as was prescribed above.*

*At the Praises, we set 4 verses, and we sing the resurrectional stichera:*

*Tone 4:*

O Thou Who didst endure the Cross and death, and didst rise from the dead, O Almighty Lord, we glorify Thy Resurrection.

By Thy Cross, O Christ, Thou hast freed us from the ancient curse; and by Thy Death Thou hast abolished the devil who tormented our nature; and by Thy Rising Thou hast filled all things with joy: therefore we cry aloud unto Thee: O Lord Who didst rise from the dead, glory to Thee!

By Thy Cross, O Christ Savior, guide us unto Thy truth, and deliver us from the snares of the enemy: O Thou Who didst rise from the dead, raise us up who have fallen into sin, stretching forth Thy hand, O Lord Lover of mankind, by the prayers of Thy saints.

Without departing from Thy Father's bosom, O Only-Begotten Word of God, Thou didst come down to earth out of love for mankind, becoming immutable man; and Thou didst endure the Cross and death in the flesh, being impassible in Thy Godhead; and having risen from the dead, Thou didst grant immortality unto the human race, as the only Almighty One.

*Then the Paschal Stichera with their verses, and the customary dismissal. And the Litiya outside the monastery. The Hours according to the custom of Pascha.*

*At the Liturgy, the Antiphons of Pascha.*

*Prokeimenon of the Apostle, Tone 6:*
*I will remember Thy name in every generation and generation! (Ps. 44:18a [45:17a])*
*Verse: Hear, O daughter, and see, and incline thine ear! (Ps. 44:11a [45:10a])*

*The Reading from the Acts of the Apostles, pericope 5 (Acts 2:22-36):*
*Peter said unto the people...*

*Alleluia, Tone 2:*
*My soul doth magnify the Lord, and my spirit hath rejoiced in God my Savior. (Luke 1:46b-47)*
*Verse: For He hath regarded the lowliness of His handmaiden: for behold, from henceforth all generations shall call me blessed. (Luke 1:48)*

*The Reading from the Holy Gospel according to John, pericope 6 (John 1:35-51):*
*John stood...*

*Kinonikon [Communion Hymn]:*
*Receive ye the Body of Christ; taste ye of the immortal Fountain.*

## On Wednesday of Bright Week, at Vespers[^436]

*At the Lucernarium, after "Christ is risen from the dead" and the litanies, we sing 3 resurrectional stichera, Tone 5:*

*Tone 5:*

By Thy precious Cross, O Christ, Thou hast put the devil to shame; and by Thy Resurrection Thou hast blunted the sting of sin, and hast saved us from the gates of death: we glorify Thee, O Only-Begotten One!

He Who grants resurrection unto the human race was led as a sheep to the slaughter: the princes of hades were terrified of Him, and the gates of weeping were lifted up; for Christ, the King of glory, entered in, saying unto those in bonds: Come forth! and unto those in darkness: Unveil yourselves!

A great wonder! The Creator of things invisible, having suffered in the flesh out of love for mankind, the Immortal One arose: come, ye families of the nations, let us worship Him; for having been delivered from deception by His tender mercy, we have learned to hymn the one God in three Hypostases.

*And 3 Anatolian [Eastern] Stichera, in the same Tone:*

*Sticheron:*

We offer an evening worship unto Thee, the Unwaning Light, Who at the end of the ages didst shine forth through the flesh unto the world as in a mirror, and didst descend even unto hades, and didst dispel the darkness there, and didst show the light of the Resurrection unto the nations: O Giver of light, Lord, glory to Thee!

We glorify Christ, the Author of our salvation: for when He arose from the dead, the world was saved from deception; the choir of Angels rejoices, the deception of the demons flees, fallen Adam hath arisen, the devil is abolished.

Those who were of the guard were instructed by the transgressors: Conceal the Rising of Christ, and take pieces of silver, and say: While we were asleep, the dead man was stolen from the tomb. Who hath ever seen, who hath ever heard that a dead man was stolen? Especially one anointed and naked, leaving behind his burial wrappings in the tomb! Be not deceived, O Jews: learn the sayings of the Prophets, and understand that He is truly the Redeemer of the world, and the Almighty!

*Glory:*

O Lord, Who didst despoil hades and trample down death, O our Savior, Who didst enlighten the world by Thy precious Cross: have mercy upon us!

*Both now: Theotokion (Dogmatikon), Tone 5:*

In the Red Sea there was once depicted an image of the unwedded Bride: there Moses was the divider of the water; here Gabriel was the minister of the wonder; then Israel walked dryshod through the deep;

and now the Virgin hath given birth unto Christ without seed; the sea remained impassable after the passage of Israel; the Blameless One remained incorrupt after the childbearing of Emmanuel. O God Who art and ever wast, and hast appeared as man: have mercy upon us!

*Entrance with the censer.*

*O Gladsome Light.*

*Prokeimenon, Tone 7:*

Give ear, O God, unto my prayer, and despise not my supplication! (Ps. 54:2 [55:1])

*Verse 1: Attend unto me, and hear me! (Ps. 54:3a [55:2a])*

*Verse 2: For they brought iniquity upon me. (Ps. 54:4b [55:3b])*

*Verse 3: As for me, I have cried unto God, and the Lord heard me: evening, and morning, and at noonday. (Ps. 54:17-18a [55:16-17a])*

*And again:*

Give ear, O God, unto my prayer, and despise not my supplication!

*The litany. And: "Vouchsafe, O Lord, to keep us this evening..."; and all the rest as was prescribed.*

*At the Aposticha, the resurrectional sticheron, Tone 5:*

Thee, the incarnate Savior Christ, Who wast not separated from heaven, do we magnify in the voices of hymns: for Thou didst accept the Cross and death for our race, as the Lord Lover of mankind; and having overthrown the gates of hades, Thou didst rise on the third day, saving our souls.

*Then the stichera of Pascha, with their verses as was prescribed.*

*"Christ is risen from the dead...", thrice.*

*And the dismissal.*

## On Thursday of Bright Week, at Matins[^437]

*The entire order is conducted as was prescribed.*

*At the Praises, "Let every breath...": we set 4 verses, and we sing the resurrectional stichera, Tone 5:*

*Tone 5:*

O Lord, though the tomb was sealed by the transgressors, Thou didst come forth from the tomb, just as Thou wast born of the Mother of God: Thine incorporeal Angels understood not how Thou wast incarnate; the soldiers who guarded Thee perceived not when Thou didst arise; for both were sealed unto those who inquired: but the miracles were manifested unto those who worship the mystery in faith; grant joy and great mercy unto us who hymn it!

*Sticheron:*

O Lord, Who didst shatter the eternal bars and break asunder the bonds, Thou didst rise from the tomb, leaving behind Thy burial wrappings as a witness of Thy true third-day Burial; and Thou didst go before into Galilee, Thou Who wast guarded in the cave: great is Thy mercy, O Incomprehensible Savior, have mercy and save us!

*Sticheron:*

O Lord, the women ran unto the tomb to see Thee, Christ, Who didst suffer for our sake; and having come, they found an Angel sitting upon the stone, shaken with fear, and he cried out unto them, saying: The Lord is risen, tell the disciples that He is risen from the dead, saving our souls!

*Sticheron:*

O Lord, just as Thou didst come forth from the sealed tomb, so didst Thou enter in unto Thy disciples while the doors were shut, showing them the bodily sufferings which Thou didst endure, O long-suffering Savior; as of the seed of David, Thou didst endure wounds; but as the Son of God, Thou didst free the world: great is Thy mercy, O Incomprehensible Savior, have mercy and save us!

*Then the stichera of Pascha, with their verses. "Christ is risen from the dead...", thrice; and the dismissal.*

*The Hours of Pascha.*

*At the Liturgy, the Antiphons of Pascha.*

*Prokeimenon, Tone 3:*
*Sing praises to our God, sing praises: sing praises unto our King, sing praises! (Ps. 46:7 [47:6])*
*Verse: Clap your hands, all ye nations; shout unto God with the voice of rejoicing! (Ps. 46:2 [47:1])*

*The Reading from the Acts of the Apostles, pericope 7 (Acts 2:38-43):*
*Peter said...*

*Alleluia, Tone 4:*
*Bend Thy bow, and prosper, and reign, for the sake of truth, and meekness, and righteousness! (Ps. 44:5a [45:4a])*
*Verse: Thou hast loved righteousness, and hated iniquity. (Ps. 44:8a [45:7a])*

*The Reading from the Holy Gospel according to John, pericope 8 (John 3:1-15):*
*There was a certain man of the Pharisees, named Nicodemus...*

*Kinonikon [Communion Hymn]:*
*Receive ye the Body of Christ; taste ye of the immortal Fountain.*

## On Thursday of Bright Week, at Vespers[^438]

*According to custom: "Christ is risen from the dead...", with the verses.*

*At "Lord, I have cried", we set 6 verses, and we sing 3 resurrectional stichera of the Octoechos, Tone 6:*

*Tone 6:*

Having victory over hades, O Christ, Thou didst ascend upon the Cross, that Thou mightest raise up with Thyself those who sat in the darkness of death, O Free among the dead, Who fountainest forth life from Thine own light: O Almighty Savior, have mercy upon us!

*Sticheron:*

Today Christ, having trampled down death as He said, arose, and granted rejoicing unto the world, that all of us crying out may thus say the hymn: O Fountain of life, O Unapproachable Light, Almighty Savior, have mercy upon us!

*Sticheron:*

Thee, O Lord, Who art throughout all creation, whither shall we sinners flee? In heaven Thou dwellest Thyself; in hades Thou hast trampled down death; into the depths of the sea? There is Thy hand, O Master! Unto Thee do we flee, falling down before Thee we pray: O Thou Who didst rise from the dead, have mercy upon us!

*Other Stichera, Anatolian [Eastern], in the same Tone:*

*Sticheron:*

In Thy Cross do we boast, O Christ, and Thy Resurrection we hymn and glorify: for Thou art our God, besides Thee we know none other.

*Sticheron:*

Glory to Thy power, O Lord, for Thou hast abolished him that had the dominion of death, and hast renewed us by Thy Cross, granting us life and incorruption.

*Sticheron:*

Ever blessing the Lord, we sing His Resurrection: for having endured the Cross, by Death He destroyed death.

*Glory:*

Thy Burial, O Lord, having shattered the bonds of hades, tore them asunder; by Thy Resurrection from the dead Thou didst enlighten the world: O Lord, glory to Thee!

*Both now: Theotokion (Dogmatikon), Tone 6:*

Who doth not bless thee, O Most Holy Virgin? Who will not hymn thy most pure childbearing? For the Only-Begotten Son, Who shone forth timelessly from the Father, the same came forth from thee, O Pure One, having ineffably become incarnate: being God by nature, and having become man by nature for our sake, not divided into two persons, but known in two natures without confusion; entreat Him, O Pure and All-Blessed One, that our souls may be saved!

*Entrance with the censer.*

*O Gladsome Light.*

*Great Prokeimenon, Tone 7:*

I will love Thee, O Lord, my strength: the Lord is my foundation and my refuge! (Ps. 17:2-3a [18:1-2a])

*Verse 1: My God is my helper, and I will hope in Him. (Ps. 17:3b [18:2b])*

*Verse 2: Praising I will call upon the Lord, and I shall be saved from mine enemies. (Ps. 17:4 [18:3])*

*Verse 3: He heard my voice out of His holy temple. (Ps. 17:7b [18:6b])*

*And again:*

I will love Thee, O Lord, my strength: the Lord is my foundation and my refuge!

*Let us all say: "Vouchsafe, O Lord, to keep us this evening..."; and the rest.*

*At the Aposticha, the resurrectional sticheron, Tone 6, of the Octoechos:*

Thy Resurrection, O Christ Savior, the Angels hymn in the heavens: and vouchsafe that we on earth may glorify Thee with a pure heart!

*Then the stichera of Pascha, and the rest.*

## On Friday of Bright Week, at Matins[^439]

*At the Praises, "Let every breath...": And we sing 4 resurrectional stichera of the Octoechos, Tone 6:*

*Tone 6:*

Thy Cross, O Lord, is life and resurrection unto Thy people; and trusting therein, we hymn Thee, our Risen God: have mercy upon us!

Thy Burial, O Master, opened paradise unto the human race; and having been delivered from corruption, we hymn Thee, our Risen God: have mercy upon us!

With the Father and the Spirit let us hymn Christ, Who arose from the dead, and let us cry aloud unto Him: Thou art our life and resurrection: have mercy upon us!

On the third day Thou didst rise from the tomb, O Christ, as it is written, raising our forefather with Thyself: therefore the human race glorifies Thee, and hymns Thy Resurrection.

*And the stichera of Pascha, according to their custom. The litanies, and the dismissal. And the First Hour. The Hours of Pascha.*

*At the Liturgy, the Antiphons of Pascha.*

*Prokeimenon of the Day, Tone 8:*
*Their sound is gone out into all the earth, and their words unto the ends of the world! (Ps. 18:5 [19:4])*
*Verse: The heavens declare the glory of God, and the firmament proclaims the work of His hands! (Ps. 18:2 [19:1])*

*The Reading from the Acts of the Apostles, pericope 7 (Acts 3:1-8):*
*Peter and John were going up together into the temple...*

*Alleluia, Tone 1:*
*The heavens shall confess Thy wonders, O Lord! (Ps. 88:6a [89:5a])*
*Verse: God is glorified in the council of the saints! (Ps. 88:8a [89:7a])*

*The Reading from the Holy Gospel according to John, pericope 7 (John 2:12-22):*
*Jesus went down to Capernaum...*

*Kinonikon [Communion Hymn]:*
*Receive ye the Body of Christ; taste ye of the immortal Fountain.*

## On Friday of Bright Week, at Vespers[^440]

*After "Christ is risen from the dead" and the litanies,*
*at "Lord, I have cried", we set 6 verses, and we sing the resurrectional stichera, Tone 8:*

*Tone 8:*

An evening hymn and rational worship do we offer unto Thee, O Christ: for Thou wast well pleased to have mercy upon us by Thy Resurrection.

O Lord, O Lord, cast us not away from Thy presence, but be well pleased to have mercy upon us by Thy Resurrection.

Rejoice, O holy Zion, mother of the churches, dwelling place of God: for thou wast the first to receive remission of sins through the Resurrection.

*3 Anatolian [Eastern] Stichera, in the same Tone:*

*Sticheron:*

The Word of God the Father, begotten before the ages, in the latter times the Same, having willingly become incarnate of her that knew not wedlock, endured crucifixion and death; and having of old slain man, He saved him by His Resurrection.

Thy Resurrection from the dead we glorify, O Christ, whereby Thou hast delivered the race of Adam from the tyranny of hades; and as God, Thou hast granted eternal life and great mercy unto the world.

Glory to Thee, O Christ Savior, Only-Begotten Son of God, Who wast nailed upon the Cross, and didst rise from the tomb on the third day!

*Glory:*

Thee do we glorify, O Lord, Who willingly didst endure the Cross for our sake, and before Thee we bow down, O Almighty Savior: cast us not away from Thy presence, but hear and save us by Thy Resurrection, O Lover of mankind!

*Both now: Theotokion (Dogmatikon), Tone 8:*

The King of heaven out of love for mankind appeared on earth and dwelt with men: for having taken flesh from the pure Virgin, and having come forth from her with the flesh assumed, He is one Son, twofold in nature, but not in hypostasis; therefore truly proclaiming Him to be perfect God and perfect man, we confess Christ our God: Whom do thou beseech, O unwedded Mother, that our souls may be saved!

*Entrance with the censer.*

*O Gladsome Light.*

*Prokeimenon, Tone 8:*

Thou hast given an heritage unto those that fear Thee, O Lord! (Ps. 60:6b [61:5b])

*Verse 1: From the ends of the earth have I cried unto Thee! (Ps. 60:3a [61:2a])*

*Verse 2: I will shelter myself under the shadow of Thy wings! (Ps. 60:5b [61:4b])*

*Verse 3: So will I chant unto Thy name unto the ages! (Ps. 60:9a [61:8a])*

*And again:*

Thou hast given an heritage unto those that fear Thee, O Lord!

*Then the litany. "Vouchsafe, O Lord, to keep us this evening..."; and the rest:*

*At the Aposticha, the resurrectional sticheron, Tone 8:*

Thou didst ascend upon the Cross, O Jesus, Who didst descend from heaven; Thou didst come unto death, O Immortal Life; unto those in darkness Thou art the true Light, unto all the fallen the Resurrection: O our Enlightenment and Savior, glory to Thee!

*Then the stichera of Pascha, with their verses. "Christ is risen from the dead...", thrice.*

*And the dismissal.*

## On Saturday of Bright Week, at Matins[^441]

*The entire order is conducted as was prescribed above.*

*At the Praises, the resurrectional stichera, on 4 verses, Tone 8:*

*Tone 8:*

O Lord, though Thou didst stand before the judgment seat, judged by Pilate, yet Thou didst not depart from Thy throne, sitting together with the Father; and having risen from the dead, Thou hast freed the world from the bondage of the alien, as compassionate and the Lover of mankind.

O Lord, though the Jews laid Thee in the tomb as a dead man, yet soldiers guarded Thee as a sleeping King, and as a treasure of life they sealed Thee with a seal: but Thou didst rise, and hast granted incorruption unto our souls!

O Lord, Thou hast given us Thy Cross as a weapon against the devil: for he trembles and shakes, unable to bear beholding its power; for it raises the dead, and hath abolished death: therefore we bow down before Thy Burial and Resurrection.

Thy Angel, O Lord, who proclaimed the Resurrection, struck terror into the guards, and called aloud unto the women, saying: Why seek ye the Living among the dead? He is risen, being God, and hath granted life unto the universe.

*Then the stichera of Pascha, with their verses. "Christ is risen from the dead...", thrice. The litanies, and the dismissal. And the First Hour. The First, Third, and Sixth Hours of Pascha.*

*At the Liturgy, the Antiphons of Pascha.*

*Prokeimenon of the Apostle, Tone 3:*
*The Lord is my light and my Savior: whom shall I fear? (Ps. 26:1a)*
*Verse: The Lord is the defender of my life: of whom shall I be afraid? (Ps. 26:1b)*

*The Reading from the Acts of the Apostles, pericope 8 (Acts 3:11-16):*[^442]
*As the lame man who was healed held Peter and John...*

*Alleluia, Tone 5:*
*The Lord is King, He is robed in majesty. (Ps. 92:1a)*
*Verse: For He hath established the world, so that it cannot be moved. (Ps. 92:1b)*

*The Reading from the Holy Gospel according to John, pericope 11 (John 3:22-33):*
*At that time, Jesus and His disciples came... And the rest of the Liturgy. Kinonikon [Communion Hymn]: Receive ye the Body of Christ; taste ye of the immortal Fountain.*

*After the dismissal of the Liturgy, as the brethren go from the church unto the refectory, the deacon first carries the Artos, without the icon of the Resurrection of Christ; while the paraoecclesiarch and the brethren ring all the bells at that time.*

*In the refectory, when the brethren sit down, the cellarer, having broken into pieces the bread that was elevated throughout all the Week instead of the Panagia, distributes it unto the brethren before their ordinary meal; and thus we consume it. First saying: "Christ is risen from the dead...", then: "Our Father...", and the customary blessing of the table. And sitting down, they eat. The reader begins the reading. At the table there is consolation for the brethren. And having risen from the table, we chant: "Christ is risen from the dead...", thrice; "Blessed is God Who shows mercy and feeds us..."; then: "Glory to Thee, O Lord, glory to Thee, O Holy One"; "Glory... Both now and for ever and unto ages of ages. Amen."; "Lord, have mercy", thrice; "Bless!" And the deacon, taking the ordinary bread that is elevated throughout the whole year, with three fingers, in both hands, makes the sign of the cross with it over the panagiarion, saying: "Instead of 'Great is the name of the Holy Trinity': Christ is risen!", once. And we answer: "Truly He is risen!" Then lifting the portion above the icon of the Mother of God, he says: "Most Holy Mother of God, help us!" And we say: "By her prayers, O Christ God, have mercy upon us!" And we say: "All generations call thee blessed..."; and the Heirmos of the Ninth Ode: "Shine, shine..."; with both of its troparia; and again: "Shine, shine..." Then the superior says—or if he is not present, the priest says: "Through the manifold supplications of the all-blessed Sovereign Lady..."; and we say: "By her prayers!" And the priest: "The Lord is merciful and gracious, He hath given food unto them that fear Him." And we say: "Christ is risen from the dead...", thrice; the Trisagion; after "Our Father...": Hypakoe: "Anticipating the morning..."; "Glory... Both now and for ever and unto ages of ages. Amen.": "Though Thou didst descend into the tomb..."; "Lord, have mercy", thrice; "Bless!" "Blessed is God Who shows mercy and feeds us from His rich gifts!" And we say: "Amen."*

*And the customary mutual forgiveness.*

*And thus we do until the Apodosis of the Feast of Pascha.*

---

### Notes

[^16]: Codicological Landmark Note: In this 1907 Lviv Stauropegion edition of the Flowery Triodion (*Triod Tsvitnaya* / Pentecostarion), the primary liturgical text opens on printed page 1 (physical leaf p9) with Friday of Palm Week (the eve of Lazarus Saturday), continuing through Lazarus Saturday, Palm Sunday, and Great and Holy Passion Week before reaching Holy Pascha on page 198 (physical leaf p206). In accordance with the publication specification and Master Translation Standard MTS-1, Part I opens here at the inception of the liturgical text.
[^17]: Emperor Leo VI the Wise (866–912), Byzantine emperor and hymnographer, authored the series of Idiomela (*samoglasny*) for Lazarus Saturday chanting the resurrection of Lazarus as the pledge of the universal resurrection.
[^18]: The vesperal reading on Friday of the 6th Week of the Great Fast (eve of Lazarus Saturday) is from Genesis 49:33–50:26 according to the Septuagint (LXX) text, recounting the death and embalming of Jacob in Egypt and Joseph's oath to bury him in the land of Canaan.
[^19]: The second reading at Vespers on Friday of Palm Week is the Praise of the Valiant Woman (LXX Proverbs 31:8–31 / MT 31:10–31), opening with the counsel to open one's mouth in defense of the poor and weak, prefiguring the pious women (Martha and Mary) who served the Lord and received Lazarus back from the tomb.
[^20]: Rubrical note on leaf p12: From this day forward (Friday of Palm Week), neither the Martyrikon (*Мꙋ́чениченъ*), nor the Theotokion (*Бг҃оро́диченъ*), nor the Octoechos (*Ѻ҆смогла́сникъ*) is chanted until the Saturday of Saint Thomas (Antipascha), marking the complete cessation of the weekly Octoechos cycle for the duration of Passion Week and Bright Week.
[^21]: The liturgical rubric notes the divergence between the general Byzantine monastic practice appointing Great Compline (*Повече́рїе вели́кое*) and the specific Ruthenian regional usage established at the Synod of Lviv (1891), which appointed Small Compline (*Повече́рїе ма́лое*) together with the Canon of Saint Andrew of Crete for the eve of Lazarus Saturday.
[^22]: Saint Andrew of Crete (ca. 660–740), Archbishop of Gortyna and author of the Great Canon of Repentance, is surnamed "the Hierosolymite" (*І҆еросолими́тъ*) in reference to his monastic tonsure and early ascetic formation at the Lavra of Saint Sabbas the Sanctified in Jerusalem.
[^23]: Lazarus Saturday (*Суббѡ́та ст҃а́гѡ и҆ пра́веднагѡ Ла́зарѧ*) commemorates the raising of the four-days-dead Lazarus of Bethany (John 11:1–45) by Our Lord Jesus Christ as the solemn pledge and prefiguration of the universal resurrection before His own voluntary Passion and death.
[^24]: The Midnight Office rubrics for Lazarus Saturday prescribe the festal troparion (*Confirming the common resurrection...*) and kontakion (*Christ, the joy of all...*), omitting the customary penitential prayers for the departed (*Помянѝ, Го́споди...*).
[^25]: The chanting of the Resurrectional Troparia of the Blameless (*Еѵлогїта́рїа* / *А҆́ггельскїй собо́ръ*, "Blessed art Thou, O Lord: teach me Thy statutes") on a Saturday is a unique liturgical occurrence found only on Lazarus Saturday and Great and Holy Saturday, testifying to the intensely resurrectional character of the day.
[^26]: The Matins Gospel is omitted on Lazarus Saturday except in a church dedicated to Saint Lazarus. The appointed patristic reading consists of the homiletic commentary on John 11 by Saint John Chrysostom (*Homilies 62 and 63 on the Gospel of John*, designated in the Slavonic text as chapters 33 and 34 of the commentary).
[^27]: The Matins canon combines two compositions: the first by Saint Theophanes the Branded (*Theophanes Graptos*, ca. 778–845), Metropolitan of Nicaea; the second by Saint John Damascene. From Ode 6, the office incorporates the four-ode canon (*четверопѣ́снецъ*) of Saint Cosmas of Maiuma.
[^28]: "John the Monk" (*І҆ѡа́ннъ мона́хъ*) is the standard liturgical designation for Saint John of Damascus (Damascene, ca. 676–749), monk of the Lavra of Saint Sabbas and Doctor of the Church.
[^29]: The Sessional Hymn model *Tone 8, Podoben: "The Wisdom..." (Премꙋ́дрости)* is the celebrated poetic incipit *Τὴν Σοφίαν καὶ Λόγον ἐν σῇ γαστρὶ συλλαβοῦσα* ("Having conceived the Wisdom and the Word in thy womb"), attributed to Saint Theodore the Studite or Saint John Damascene, widely utilized across the Triodion and Pentecostarion for festive sessional compositions.
[^30]: Saint Cosmas the Melodist, Bishop of Maiuma (ca. 675–752), foster brother of Saint John Damascene, composed the four-ode canon (*четверопѣ́снецъ*) spanning Odes 6 through 9, which forms the concluding portion of the Matins canon for Lazarus Saturday.
[^31]: The Kontakion model *Tone 2, Podoben: "Seeking the things on high" (Вы́шнихъ и҆щѧ̀)* (*Τὰ ἄνω ζητῶν*) is Saint Romanos the Melodist's classical prooimion model, here applied to Lazarus Saturday to proclaim Christ as the universal Joy, Truth, and Resurrection of all.
[^32]: The liturgical Synaxarion for Lazarus Saturday from the historical compendium of Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos (ca. 1256–1335). It synthesizes patristic traditions concerning Lazarus's life after his resurrection, including his flight to Kition in Cyprus to escape the Sanhedrin, his thirty-year episcopate, the hand-woven omophorion gifted him by the Theotokos, and the translation of his relics to Constantinople under Emperor Leo VI the Wise (reigned 886–912) in 899.
[^33]: In place of the Trisagion (*Свѧты́й Бж҃е*), the baptismal hymn from Galatians 3:27 (*"As many of you as have been baptized into Christ have put on Christ. Alleluia"*) is sung at the Divine Liturgy on Lazarus Saturday. In the ancient cathedral usage of Constantinople and Jerusalem, this day was one of the primary baptismal vigils of the liturgical year, when catechumens were solemnly baptized into Christ prior to Holy Week.
[^34]: Monastic dietary mitigation on Lazarus Saturday: the Great Fast rule of xerophagy is mitigated to permit cooked food with olive oil (*варе́нїемъ со є҆ле́емъ*) and wine. Furthermore, the consumption of fish roe/caviar (*и҆кра̀*) is explicitly permitted up to three ounces (*трѝ ѻ҆́нгїи*), while whole fish remains strictly prohibited until Palm Sunday.
[^35]: The rubrical statute for a parish or monastery temple dedicated to Saint Lazarus directs the chanting of the Polyeleos at Matins, the First Antiphon of the Anavathmoi (Hymns of Ascent) in Tone 4, and the full patristic reading of the Life of Saint Lazarus composed by Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos.
[^36]: Small Vespers on the Eve of Palm Sunday (*на ма́лѣй вече́рни въ сꙋббѡ́тꙋ ва́їй ве́чера*) is celebrated before sunset in monastic communities, preceding the solemn festal Great Vespers. Its stichera anticipate the Entry of the Lord into Jerusalem riding upon the foal of an ass.
[^37]: Great Vespers on Saturday Evening begins the Great Feast of the Palm Sunday of the Lord's Entry into Jerusalem (*Вхо́дъ Гдⷭ҇ень въ І҆ерꙋсали́мъ* / *Недѣ́лѧ Ва́їй*), one of the Twelve Great Feasts (*Dodekaorton*). The opening sticheron, *"Today the grace of the Holy Spirit hath gathered us together..."* (*Дне́сь бл҃года́ть ст҃а́гѡ Дх҃а на́съ собра̀*), is one of the most renowned processional idiomela in Byzantine hymnography.
[^38]: The Old Testament Paremias at Palm Sunday Great Vespers: Genesis 49:1-2, 8-12 (the Patriarch Jacob's dying blessing upon Judah, prophesying the perpetual royal sceptre, the expectation of the nations, and the King binding His foal unto the vine and washing His garments in the blood of the grape); Zephaniah 3:14-19 (the joyous advent of the King of Israel in Zion, removing judgment and casting out enemies); and Zechariah 9:9-15 (the messianic prophecy of the meek King entering Jerusalem mounted upon the foal of an ass, cutting off chariot and war-bow, and establishing universal peace).
[^39]: The festal dismissal troparia of Palm Sunday: The primary troparion, *Tone 1: "Giving assurance of the general resurrection before Thy Passion, Thou didst raise Lazarus from the dead, O Christ our God; therefore we also, like the children, carrying the tokens of victory, cry aloud to Thee, the Vanquisher of death: Hosanna in the highest! Blessed is He that comes in the name of the Lord"* (chanted twice), and the baptismal troparion, *Tone 4: "Having been buried with Thee through baptism, O Christ our God, we have been made worthy of immortal life through Thy Resurrection; and chanting we cry aloud: Hosanna in the highest! Blessed is He that comes in the name of the Lord"* (chanted once).
[^40]: Physical Leaf p26 (fol. 21r).
[^41]: The solemn Prayer for the Blessing of Palms (*Моли́тва на бл҃гослове́нїе ва́їа*) recited by the priest at Matins after the chanting of the festal Gospel and Psalm 50, followed by the ceremonial distribution of blessed palms and willow branches (*ва́їа и҆ вѣ́тви древе́съ*) to the clergy and faithful.
[^42]: The Canon of Palm Sunday Matins composed by Saint Cosmas the Melodist, Bishop of Maiuma (ca. 675–752). The Greek acrostic formed by the initial letters of the troparia across Odes 1 through 9 reads: *Ὡσαννὰ Χριστὸς εὐλογημένος Θεός* ("Hosanna, Christ is blessed God").
[^43]: The Ikos of Palm Sunday draws a theological contrast between the slaughter of the Innocents in Bethlehem at Christ's Nativity and His voluntary self-offering upon the Cross for all humanity, both young and old.
[^44]: The Synaxarion for Palm Sunday (*Вхо́дъ Гдⷭ҇ень въ І҆ерꙋсали́мъ* / *Ἡ Κυριακὴ τῶν Βαΐων*) by Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos (ca. 1256–1335). It expounds the historical circumstances of the Lord's royal entry into Jerusalem six days before the Passover, the typology of the palms (*ва́їа*) as emblems of victory over death, and the foal of an ass as the prefiguration of the Gentile nations called from idolatrous irrationality into the Church of God.
[^45]: At Ode 9 of the Matins Canon on Palm Sunday, the biblical canticle of the Theotokos (*Magnificat* / *Честнѣ́йшꙋю*) is omitted in accordance with the rubrics for Master Feasts of the Lord (*Владычніи праздники*), proceeding immediately to the festal heirmos.
[^46]: The Festive Antiphons of Palm Sunday at the Divine Liturgy are taken from Psalms 114, 115, and 117 (LXX). Each antiphon features its own proper festal refrain celebrating Christ's entry upon the foal.
[^47]: The Festal Entrance Hymn (*Входное*) at the Little Entrance of the Divine Liturgy is taken from Psalm 117:26-27a: *"Blessed is He that comes in the name of the Lord; we have blessed you out of the house of the Lord; God is the Lord, and hath appeared unto us."*
[^48]: Monastic dietary mitigation on Palm Sunday: Although occurring within Great Lent, because the Entry of the Lord into Jerusalem is one of the Twelve Great Feasts, the typikon mitigates the strict Lenten fast to permit the consumption of fish, wine, and oil (*ꙗ҆ди́мъ ры́бы, и҆ вїно̀ пїе́мъ*).
[^49]: Palm Sunday Evening Vespers opens Great and Holy Week and marks the liturgical transition from the festal triumph of the Entry into Jerusalem to the solemn, sober anticipation of the Lord's Sufferings and the Bridegroom Matins services (*Служба Жениха*).
[^50]: Palm Sunday Evening dismissal order: The penitential conclusion of Palm Sunday Vespers marks the formal entrance into the severe fast and solemnity of Great and Holy Passion Week (*Страстная Седмица*), reintroducing the great prostrations with the prayer of St. Ephrem the Syrian and the penitential troparia (*"Rejoice, O Virgin Theotokos"* / *Богоро́дице Дѣ́во ра́дꙋйсѧ*).
[^51]: The Compline Tri-ode of Saint Andrew of Crete: Throughout Great Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday, Small Compline features a three-ode canon (tri-ode / *трипѣ́снецъ*) composed by Saint Andrew of Crete (ca. 660–740), Archbishop of Gortyna, meditating on the impending voluntary Passion of Christ and the typological figures of the day (Joseph the All-Comely and the Barren Fig Tree).
[^52]: The reading of the Psalter during Passion Week: In accordance with ancient Studite and Sabaite typika, the entire Psalter (all 20 kathismata) is read once through between Monday and Wednesday noon (three kathismata at Matins, and one each at the Third and Sixth Hours), ceasing completely after the Presanctified Divine Liturgy of Great Wednesday.
[^53]: The 17th Kathisma (Psalm 118 LXX / *Непоро́чны*) is reserved for Great and Holy Saturday Matins at the Burial of Christ (the Lamentations / *Погребе́нїе Хрїсто́во*), while Kathisma 18 (the Gradual Psalms 119–133 LXX) is sung at Vespers throughout Great Lent and Holy Week.
[^54]: The Bridegroom Matins (*Служба Жениха*) of Great and Holy Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday: Celebrated by anticipation on the preceding evening in parish and monastic practice. The solemn Troparion *"Behold, the Bridegroom comes at midnight"* (*Се́ Жени́хъ гряде́тъ въ полꙋ́нощи*) derives from the Parable of the Ten Virgins (Matthew 25:1–13) and serves as the thematic anthem of the opening days of Passion Week.
[^55]: Patristic readings at Bridegroom Matins: The appointed homiletic commentaries of Saint John Chrysostom on the Gospel of Matthew (specifically Homilies 67 and 68, expounding the withering of the barren fig tree and the cleansing of the Temple).
[^56]: Saint John Chrysostom's Homily on the Withered Fig Tree (*Сло́во о и҆зсо́хшей смоко́вницѣ*), examining the mystical significance of the curse pronounced upon the leafy, fruitless tree as a prophetic judgment upon the formalist hypocrisy of the Old Covenant leadership.
[^57]: Saint John Damascene's Homily on the Withered Fig Tree and the Parable of the Wicked Vinedressers (Matthew 21:33–44).
[^58]: The Matins Tri-ode of Great Monday composed by Saint Cosmas the Melodist, Bishop of Maiuma (ca. 675–752), bearing the Greek alphabetical acrostic *Τῇ δευτέρᾳ* ("On the Second Day," i.e., Monday), spanning Odes 1, 8, and 9.
[^59]: The Synaxarion for Great and Holy Monday by Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos (ca. 1256–1335), commemorating two foundational types: the Patriarch Joseph the All-Comely (*І҆ѡ́сифъ прекра́сный*) as a prefiguration of Christ's betrayal, descent into the pit, and exaltation as Ruler and Giver of the Bread of Life; and the cursed and withered fig tree (*и҆зсо́хшая смоко́вница*) as an emblem of spiritual barrenness and hypocrisy.
[^60]: Saint Isidore of Pelusium (ca. 360–435 or 450), ascetic abbot and prolific theologian in Egypt, who in his *Epistles* (Book I, Letter 153) recorded the ancient patristic tradition identifying the Tree of the Knowledge of Good and Evil in Paradise as a fig tree, whose leaves Adam and Eve used to sew aprons after the Fall.
[^61]: The celebrated Exapostilarion (Hymn of Light / *Свѣти́ленъ*) of Passion Week, *"Thy bridal chamber I behold adorned, O my Savior..."* (*Черто́гъ тво́й ви́ждꙋ, Спа́се мо́й*), chanted thrice by a soloist in the centre of the church while the congregation kneels, expressing the soul's yearning for the heavenly bridal banquet and its acute unworthiness.
[^62]: The proper dismissal formula for Great Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday: *"The Lord, going unto His voluntary Passion for our salvation, Christ our true God..."* (*Гряды́й Госпо́дь на во́льнꙋю Стра́сть, на́шегѡ ра́ди спасе́нїя, Христо́съ и҆́стинный Бо́гъ на́шъ...*).
[^63]: The continuous reading of the Four Gospels (*Четвероева́нгелїе*) during the Hours of Great Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday: The entirety of the four Evangelists (Matthew, Mark, and Luke complete; John read up to 13:31) is solemnly proclaimed from an adorned analogion positioned in the nave, completing the full Gospel witness before the commemorative enactment of the Lord's Passion.
[^64]: The Sixth Hour prophecy on Great Monday: Ezekiel 1:1–20, recounting the Prophet Ezekiel's vision of the divine chariot-throne (*Merkavah*) by the river Chebar, with the four winged living creatures (the Tetramorph) bearing the likenesses of a man, a lion, an ox, and an eagle—patristically interpreted by Saint Irenaeus of Lyons and Saint Jerome as the four Evangelists.
[^65]: Codicological Note on the Passion Gospels Rubric: The text notes the liturgical distribution of the Four Gospels across the first three days of Great Week, preparing for the proclamation of the Twelve Passion Gospels at Great Thursday Matins.
[^66]: The continuous reading of the Four Gospels (*Четвероева́нгелїе*) during Holy Week: Matthew, Mark, and Luke are read in their entirety, while John is read up to the discourse on the Passion (John 13:31). The readings are distributed across nine sessions during the Third, Sixth, and Ninth Hours of Great Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday, concluding at the Ninth Hour of Great Wednesday before the Presanctified Divine Liturgy.
[^67]: The liturgical order of the Ninth Hour on Great Monday through Wednesday omits the customary kathisma psalmody (*стїхосло́вїе*). The service proceeds directly from the three fixed Psalms to the Troparion of the Ninth Hour, followed by the Gospel proclamation from the adorned analogion in the center of the nave.
[^68]: At Great Monday Presanctified Vespers, the appointed stichera on *Lord, I have cried* reprise the solemn idiomel hymns of Monday Matins (*"The Lord, going unto His voluntary Passion..."*), emphasizing the journey toward Jerusalem and the voluntary character of Christ's sacrifice.
[^69]: The Old Testament reading from Exodus 1:1–20 at Presanctified Vespers recounts the enslavement and affliction of Israel under Pharaoh in Egypt. In patristic typology, Pharaoh figures the devil, Egypt represents the tyranny of sin, and the brick-making servitude prefigures humanity's spiritual bondage prior to Christ's redemption.
[^70]: Heliopolis (On / *Ὢν, ἥ ἐστιν Ἡλιούπολις*): Exodus 1:11 in the Septuagint explicitly witnesses three fortified treasure cities built for Pharaoh—Pithom, Raamses, and On (Heliopolis)—whereas the Masoretic Text names only Pithom and Raamses.
[^71]: The Old Testament reading from Job 1:1–12 at Presanctified Vespers presents the righteous Job as an innocent sufferer whose sudden and severe trials prefigure the unmerited sufferings, passion, and eventual vindication of Christ.
[^72]: The ancient Studite-Sabaite fasting rule prescribes xerophagy (*сꙋхоꙗде́нїе*—eating only uncooked or dried food, bread, water, and raw or salt-preserved vegetables without oil or wine) for the first three days of Passion Week, mirroring the severe ascetic discipline of the First Week of Great Lent.
[^73]: Canon 89 of the Sixth Ecumenical Council (In Trullo, 692) directs the faithful to spend the days of the saving Passion in fasting, prayer, and contrition of heart, terminating the Paschal fast at midnight of Great Saturday, in accordance with the Gospel testimonies of Matthew (28:1) and Luke (24:1).
[^74]: Great Compline on Holy Monday evening features a three-ode canon (tri-ode / *трипѣ́снецъ*) by Saint Andrew of Crete (Odes 2, 8, and 9), meditating on Christ's eschatological discourse on the Mount of Olives (Matthew 24–25).
[^75]: The Parable of the Millstones (*же́рновная при́тча*) refers to Matthew 24:41 and Luke 17:35: "Two women shall be grinding at the mill; the one shall be taken, and the other left," allegorically interpreted by the Church Fathers as distinguishing those who work with spiritual vigilance from those who labor in earthly distraction.
[^76]: Matins of Great and Holy Tuesday centers on the Parable of the Ten Virgins (Matthew 25:1–13) and the Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25:14–30), calling the soul to active vigilance, spiritual sobriety, and works of mercy before the imminent arrival of the Heavenly Bridegroom.
[^77]: Codicological cross-reference to printed folio 37 (Physical Leaf p42 = Printed Folio 37 + 5), where the Troparion of the Bridegroom (*"Behold, the Bridegroom comes at midnight..."* / *Се́ Жени́хъ гряде́тъ*) is given in full at the opening of Passion Week on Great Monday.
[^78]: The Synaxarion for Great and Holy Tuesday, composed by Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos (ca. 1256–1335), expounds the Parable of the Ten Virgins, demonstrating that bodily virginity and ascetic rigor without the oil of mercy, compassion, and active charity cannot open the gates of the heavenly bridal chamber.
[^79]: Patristic teaching on virginity and almsgiving: The synaxarist invokes Saint Isidore of Pelusium and Saint John Chrysostom in teaching that nothing is more disgraceful than virginity overcome by avarice or stinginess toward the poor.
[^80]: The Matins Di-ode of Great Tuesday, composed by Saint Cosmas the Melodist, Bishop of Maiuma (ca. 675–752), consists of Odes 8 and 9 and carries the Greek alphabetical acrostic *Τρίτῃ τε* ("And on the Third Day"), celebrating the adornment of spiritual lamps with the oil of charity.
[^81]: The solemn Exapostilarion (Hymn of Light / *Свѣти́ленъ*) of Passion Week, *"Thy bridal chamber I behold adorned, O my Savior..."* (*Черто́гъ тво́й ви́ждꙋ, Спа́се мо́й*), chanted thrice at the conclusion of the canon while the church is in darkness or lit only by candlelight.
[^82]: The Sixth Hour prophecy on Great Tuesday: Ezekiel 1:21–28, continuing the Prophet Ezekiel's vision of the chariot-throne (*Merkavah*), the crystalline firmament, the sound of wings like the Almighty (*El Shaddai*), and the sapphire throne bearing the appearance of the Glory of the Lord.
[^83]: The conclusion of the Sixth Hour prophecy on Great Tuesday: Ezekiel 1:21–28, recounting the vision of the four living creatures, the crystalline firmament, the sound of the wings like the voice of the Almighty (*Shaddai*), the sapphire throne, and the appearance of the likeness of the Glory of the Lord.
[^84]: The liturgical order of the Ninth Hour on Great Tuesday omits the customary kathisma psalmody (*стїхосло́вїе*). The service proceeds directly to the Troparion of the Ninth Hour and the Gospel reading, concluding with the Beatitudes (*Блаже́нни*) and prostrations before the Presanctified Divine Liturgy.
[^85]: The first Old Testament reading at Great Tuesday Presanctified Vespers: Exodus 2:5–10, recounting the deliverance of the infant Moses from the waters of the Nile by Pharaoh's daughter, prefiguring Christ's deliverance from the wrath of earthly rulers and the salvation of mankind through the waters of Baptism.
[^86]: The second Old Testament reading at Great Tuesday Presanctified Vespers: Job 1:13–22, describing the sudden and total loss of Job's possessions, servants, and children, and his blameless submission to divine providence (*"The Lord gave, and the Lord hath taken away; blessed be the name of the Lord"*).
[^87]: The Gospel reading at Great Tuesday Presanctified Vespers: Matthew 26:6–16 (section 120), recounting the anointing of Christ by the woman with the alabaster box of costly spikenard in the house of Simon the leper in Bethany, contrasted with Judas Iscariot's covenant with the chief priests to betray Him for thirty pieces of silver.
[^88]: Great Compline on Great Tuesday evening features a tri-ode (*трипѣ́снецъ*) composed by Saint Andrew of Crete (ca. 660–740), consisting of Odes 3, 8, and 9, meditating on spiritual vigilance, the suddenness of the Bridegroom's return, and the repentant anointing by the sinful woman.
[^89]: The solemn Sessional Hymn at Great Compline, Tone 1, Podoben: "The angelic choir" (*Ли́къ а҆́гг҃льскїй*), contemplating the lawless Sanhedrin—Annas, Caiaphas, Herod, and Pilate—gathering in hatred to examine and condemn the Long-suffering Lord of all.
[^90]: Matins of Holy and Great Wednesday centers on two diametrically opposed figures: the repentant harlot who poured out costly myrrh upon the Lord's feet to obtain the remission of her sins, and Judas Iscariot who, blinded by avarice, sold his Master to the chief priests for thirty pieces of silver.
[^91]: Codicological cross-reference to printed folio 37 (Physical Leaf p42 = Printed Folio 37 + 5), where the solemn Troparion of the Bridegroom (*"Behold, the Bridegroom comes at midnight..."* / *Се́ Жени́хъ гряде́тъ*) is given in full at the opening of Passion Week on Great Monday.
[^92]: The Matins Gospel of Holy and Great Wednesday: John 12:17–50 (sections 41 to 43), recounting the testimony of the crowds regarding the raising of Lazarus, the Greeks seeking Jesus, the Father's voice from heaven, Christ's prophecy of being lifted up upon the Cross, and His solemn warning to walk while they have the Light.
[^93]: The Matins Tri-ode of Holy and Great Wednesday, composed by Saint Cosmas the Melodist, Bishop of Maiuma (ca. 675–752), consisting of Odes 3, 8, and 9, bearing the Greek acrostic *Τετράδι ψάλλω* ("On Wednesday I sing" / *Тетра́ди ѱалῶ*).
[^94]: The Kontakion of Holy and Great Wednesday, Tone 4, Podoben: "Thou Who wast lifted up upon the Cross" (*Вознесы́йсѧ*), voicing the soul's profound sorrow for having exceeded the harlot in sinfulness without offering tears, yet begging for forgiveness through silent supplication.
[^95]: The Synaxarion of Holy and Great Wednesday, composed by Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos (ca. 1256–1335): An extensive patristic treatise harmonizing the differing Gospel accounts of the women who anointed the Lord with myrrh.
[^96]: Patristic harmonization of Saint John Chrysostom (Homily 80 on Matthew): Chrysostom teaches that there were two distinct women: the repentant sinful woman of Matthew, Mark, and Luke, and Mary the sister of Lazarus in John. Nikephoros Kallistos also discusses the opinion of other patristic commentators that three distinct anointings occurred: the repentant sinner in Galilee (Luke 7), Mary of Bethany six days before Pascha (John 12), and another woman in the house of Simon the leper two days before Pascha (Matthew 26, Mark 14).
[^98]: Patristic tradition concerning the Upper Room (*го́рница по́стлана*): Xanthopoulos distinguishes the two suburban Bethany banquets from the Last Supper, noting that the furnished upper room inside Jerusalem on Mount Zion served not only for the Mystical Supper, but also as the refuge of the Apostles after the Crucifixion, the site of the post-Resurrection appearances, the touching of Saint Thomas, and the Descent of the Holy Spirit at Pentecost.
[^97]: Epiphanius of Salamis (*De Mensuris et Ponderibus*) and patristic realia: The alabaster box (*а҆лава́стръ*, Greek *ἀλάβαστρον*) was a narrow-necked glass or stone vial without handles (*вѵ́кїонъ*, Greek *βικίον*), designed to preserve volatile ointments; *nard pistic* (*на́рдъ пістико́съ*, Greek *νάρδος πιστική*) denotes genuine, pure, unadulterated spikenard.
[^99]: The Praises (Lauds) of Holy and Great Wednesday Matins: Four idiomel stichera in Tone 1, vividly contrasting the weeping harlot untying her hair and washing the Savior's feet with Judas Iscariot plotting betrayal and calculating thirty pieces of silver.
[^100]: The Aposticha of Holy and Great Wednesday Matins: Solemn idiomel stichera in Tone 6, meditating on Christ's boundless condescension in dining at the house of the Pharisee and accepting the sinful woman's tears, while Judas extends his hands to receive the price of blood.
[^101]: The celebrated Idiomel of Saint Kassiani the Nun (Kassia, ca. 810–865), chanted at Holy Wednesday Matins and Presanctified Vespers: Tone 8, *"Lord, the woman who had fallen into many sins..."* (*Господи, яже во многїя грѣхи впадшая жена*). One of the most famous liturgical compositions of the Byzantine and Slavonic traditions, it poignantly portrays the weeping sinful woman anointing Christ's feet in anticipation of His burial, contrasting her repentance with Eve's terror in the Garden of Eden.
[^102]: Codicological cross-reference to Great Monday (Physical Leaf p46 = Printed Folio 41 + 5), where the full liturgical order of the Hours (Third, Sixth, and Ninth Hours) with kathisma psalmody, Gospel readings, and the Prayer of Saint Ephraim the Syrian is detailed in full.
[^103]: The Sixth Hour Old Testament reading of Holy and Great Wednesday: Ezekiel 2:1–3:3, recounting the prophet Ezekiel's divine vocation to preach repentance to the rebellious and stiff-hearted house of Israel, and his eating of the scroll inscribed with lamentation and woe, which tasted in his mouth sweet as honey.
[^104]: The Gospel reading at the Sixth Hour on Holy and Great Wednesday: John 12:17–50, concluding the third of the four daily Gospel lessons of Passion Week.
[^105]: The Ninth Hour of Holy and Great Wednesday marks the definitive conclusion of the reading of the Four Gospels (*четвероевангелїе*) that began on Great Monday. The final reading covers the Passion narrative up to the betrayal.
[^106]: The concluding monastic prayer of the Ninth Hour: *"O Master of great mercy, Lord Jesus Christ our God, Who through Thine ineffable love for mankind..."* (*Владыко многомилостиве*), during which the entire monastic brotherhood remains prostrated on the ground, culminating in the solemn mutual Rite of Forgiveness (*Чинъ прощенїя*) that formally marks the conclusion of the Forty Days of Great Lent.
[^107]: The reciprocal monastic forgiveness formula: each monk approaches the Superior and fellow brethren two by two, bowing to the earth and asking: *"Forgive me, holy father, a sinner, wherein I have sinned against thee in word, deed, or thought."*
[^108]: Codicological and typikon distinction: In monasteries, the brethren disperse to their cells after the mutual forgiveness to maintain absolute prayerful silence until Vespers; in cathedral and parish churches, however, the faithful remain assembled as the bells ring a festive chime (*трезвонъ*) signaling the immediate transition to Presanctified Vespers.
[^109]: The ten stichera on *"Lord, I have cried"* at Presanctified Vespers of Holy and Great Wednesday: five idiomel stichera repeated to make ten (including repetitions of the Matins Praises and Aposticha), focusing intensely on the moral and spiritual contrast between the repentant sinful woman who offered costly spikenard and Judas Iscariot who bartered his Master for thirty pieces of silver.
[^110]: The solemn repetition of Saint Kassiani's Idiomel at Vespers, Tone 8, serving as the Doxastikon (*Слава, и нынѣ*) during the Entrance with the Holy Gospel at the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts.
[^111]: The Entrance with the Holy Gospel (*Входъ со Евангелїемъ*) at Presanctified Vespers: prescribed on Holy Wednesday because of the solemn Gospel reading (Matthew 26:6–16) appointed for the Presanctified Liturgy.
[^112]: The first Old Testament reading at Holy Wednesday Presanctified Vespers: Exodus 2:11–22, recounting Moses slaying the Egyptian taskmaster who was beating a Hebrew, his flight into Midian, his defense of the daughters of Jethro (Reuel) at the well, and his marriage to Zipporah.
[^113]: The second Old Testament reading at Holy Wednesday Presanctified Vespers: Job 2:1–10 (spanning physical leaves p75 and p76), recounting the second celestial assembly, Satan obtaining permission to afflict Job's flesh with grievous boils, and Job sitting upon the ashes outside the city.
[^114]: Septuagintal and recensional note on Job 2:9 LXX: The Slavonic printed text preserves the extended Greek Septuagint reading of the desperate lamentation of Job's wife (*"How long wilt thou endure... speak some word against the Lord, and die!"*), which is absent from the Masoretic Hebrew text. The bracketed clause `[и не даде безумїя Богу]` represents the canonical LXX ending of Job 2:10 (*"and he did not charge God foolishly"* / *οὐδὲ ἔδωκεν ἀφροσύνην τῷ Θεῷ*).
[^115]: The Presanctified Gospel of Holy and Great Wednesday: Matthew 26:6–16 (section 150), detailing the anointing of Christ by the woman in the house of Simon the leper in Bethany, and Judas Iscariot negotiating His betrayal with the chief priests for thirty pieces of silver.
[^116]: The liturgical cessation of prostrations: The three great prostrations made after the transfer of the Presanctified Gifts mark the solemn cessation of all full prostrations (*метанія великїя*) within the church temple until the Kneeling Prayers of Pentecost Vespers (with the sole exception of prostrations before the Holy Epitaphios and the Cross). In private cell devotions, prostrations continue until Great Friday.
[^117]: Small Compline on Holy Wednesday evening features a tri-ode (*трипѣснецъ*) composed by Saint Andrew of Crete (ca. 660–740), consisting of Odes 4, 8, and 9, dedicated to the mysteries of Great Thursday: the preparation of the Upper Room, the Mystical Supper, the Washing of the Feet, and the contrast between the true disciples and the betrayal of Judas.
[^118]: The Sessional Hymn at Compline, Tone 4, Podoben: "Thou hast appeared today" (*Явился еси днесь* / *Ἐπεφάνης σήμερον*), meditating on the institution of the Holy Eucharist as the mystical prefiguration of Christ's sacrifice on the Cross.
[^119]: Typikon rubric on the Midnight Office: From Holy Wednesday evening onward, the Midnight Office (*Полунощница*) is entirely omitted from public church celebrations throughout Passion Week and Bright Week, resuming only on the Sunday of Saint Thomas (Antipascha).
[^120]: Codicological and iconographic description of the full-width woodcut engraving on fol. 75r (Physical Leaf p80) depicting the Washing of the Feet (Mandatum / *Умовенїе ногъ*): Christ, kneeling and girded with a linen towel, washes the feet of Saint Peter, while the other Apostles sit or stand around the table in the Upper Room of Mount Sion.
[^121]: The nocturnal striking of the semantron (*параекклисїархъ клеплетъ*) in the seventh hour of the night (approximately 1:00 AM) marks the ancient monastic schedule for Matins of Holy and Great Thursday.
[^122]: The solemn Troparion of Holy and Great Thursday Matins, Tone 8: *"When the glorious disciples were enlightened at the washing of the Supper..."* (*Егда славнїи ученицы на умовенїи Вечери просвѣщахуся*), chanted thrice with Alleluia instead of "God is the Lord," summarizing the central themes of the Day: the illumination of the Apostles, the dark madness of Judas's avarice, and Christ's righteous judgment.
[^123]: The Matins Gospel of Holy and Great Thursday: Luke 22:1–39 (beginning in section 108 and concluding within section 109), recounting the preparation of the Passover meal, the institution of the Holy Eucharist, Christ's prophecy of Peter's denial, and His departure to the Mount of Olives.
[^124]: The Canon of Holy and Great Thursday, composed by Saint Cosmas the Melodist, Bishop of Maiuma (ca. 675–752), set in Tone 6. The 1907 Lviv edition preserves the original Greek iambic acrostic: *Τῇ μακρᾷ πέμπτῃ μακρὸν ὕμνον ᾄδω* (*Тѝ макра̀ пе́мити макро́нъ Ѵ҆́мнонъ ᾄδᾄδω*, literally: "On Great Thursday I sing a long hymn"), rendered into Church Slavonic as *"Въ великїй Четвертокъ долгую пѣснь пою."*
[^125]: The Canon of Holy and Great Thursday, composed by Saint Cosmas the Melodist, Bishop of Maiuma (ca. 675–752), set in Tone 6.
