[^334]: Scriptural and dogmatic typology of incorruption: "for Thy flesh saw not corruption, O Master" (*пло́ть бо твоѧ̀ и҆стлѣ́нїа не ви́дѣ, Влⷣко*), directly citing Psalm 15:10 (LXX: *οὐδὲ δώσεις τὸν ὅσιόν σου ἰδεῖν διαφθοράν*) and the Apostle Peter's Pentecost sermon (Acts 2:27, 31), asserting the bodily incorruption of Christ in the tomb prior to the Resurrection.
[^335]: Dogma of the indivisible Hypostatic Union: "even if Thy temple was dissolved at the time of the Passion, yet even so one was the hypostasis of Thy Divinity and Thy Flesh" (*а҆́ще бо и҆ разори́сѧ тво́й хра́мъ во вре́мѧ Стра́сти, но и҆ та́кѡ є҆ди́нъ бѣ̀ соста́въ Бж҃ества̀ и҆ Пло́ти твоеѧ̀*), affirming that the separation of soul and body on the Cross did not dissolve the one composite Hypostasis of the Word incarnate.
[^336]: The Synaxarion of Holy and Great Saturday: Composed by Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos (ca. 1256–1335) for the Triodion, presenting the theological contemplation of the mystical Sabbath rest of Christ in the sepulchre, parallel to God resting on the seventh day of the primeval creation (Genesis 2:2-3).
[^337]: Patristic soteriology refuting the Satanic ransom: The Synaxarion decisively rejects the notion that Christ's Blood was paid as a ransom to the devil (*Но ни Кро́ви же взѧ́тъ вра́гъ на́шъ дїа́волъ... ка́кѡ бо а҆́ще не то́чїю ѿ Бг҃а, но и҆ Бг҃а є҆го̀ разбо́йникъ дїа́волъ ꙗ҆́ти и҆мѧ́ше;*), affirming with St. Gregory the Theologian (*Oration 45.22*) that the sacrifice was offered solely to God the Father for human redemption.
[^338]: Acrostic structure in Cosmas's odes: In Odes 7 and 8, the letters *Т-Ѡ-Н* and *М-Є-Л-П-Ѡ* are printed in the margins to continue the acrostic: *Σάββατον μέλπω* ("I sing the Sabbath"), demonstrating that Cosmas incorporated his Heirmoi into the structural sequence of the acrostic.
[^339]: The Heirmos *Lament Me not, O Mother* (*Не рыда́й менѐ, Ма́ти* / *Μὴ ἐποδύρου μου Μῆτερ*): The poetic dialogue between Christ in the tomb and the Virgin Mary, which served as the liturgical and iconographic foundation for the celebrated icon of "Extreme Humility" (*Ἄκρα Ταπείνωσις*) across the Byzantine and Slavic traditions.
[^340]: Sabbath typology of the Creation and Redemption: The Doxastikon of Praises (*Дне́шнїй де́нь та́йнѡ, вели́кїй Мѡѷсе́й проѡбразова́ше*) directly connects Genesis 2:2-3 with Christ's bodily Sabbath rest in the tomb, inaugurating the eternal Sabbath (*σαββατισμός*, Hebrews 4:9-10) of the New Covenant.
[^341]: Liturgical rubric for the Great Doxology and Procession: In the Ruthenian and Byzantine usages, during the singing of the solemn slow Trisagion of the Great Doxology, the priest in full vestments with the deacon and servers carries the Epitaphios (Holy Shroud) and the Holy Gospel in procession around the temple, representing the burial procession of Christ to the tomb.
[^342]: Maternal lament and Simeon's prophecy: The Funeral Sticheron (*Стїхи́ра надгро́бнаѧ: Прїиди́те, ᲂу҆блажи́мъ І҆ѡ́сифа приснопа́мѧтнаго*) incorporates the sorrowful lament of the Mother of God recalling the prophecy of Righteous Simeon (Luke 2:35), concluding with the threefold veneration: "We worship Thy Sufferings, O Christ, and Thy holy Resurrection!"
[^343]: Old Testament resurrection typology: The reading of Ezekiel 37:1-14 (the vision of the dry bones in the valley brought to life by the breath of the Spirit) stands as the preeminent prophetic witness of the universal bodily resurrection, chanted at the climax of Holy Saturday Matins.
[^344]: Composite liturgical Epistle lectionary: The Epistle reading (pericope 123) ingeniously fuses 1 Corinthians 5:6-8 ("Purge out therefore the old leaven... for our Pascha, Christ, is sacrificed for us") with Galatians 3:13-14 ("Christ hath redeemed us from the curse of the Law, being made a curse for us"), directly linking the Paschal sacrifice with the redemption from the curse of the tree.
[^345]: Paschaltide Lenten Hours rubric: The First Hour is sung immediately in the narthex following Matins, while the Third, Sixth, and Ninth Hours are aggregated simply in the narthex with the Beatitudes (*съ бл҃же́нными*) prior to the solemn afternoon Vespers and Liturgy of St. Basil the Great.
[^346]: Liturgical time and structure of Holy Saturday Vespers: The service is appointed for the tenth hour of the day (approximately 4:00 PM), marking the transition toward the Paschal night. It unites Vespers with the Divine Liturgy of St. Basil the Great.
[^347]: Sacerdotal vesting and Proskomedia timing: Unlike the typical Byzantine order where the Proskomedia is completed before the Liturgy begins, on Holy Saturday (as on the eve of the Nativity and Theophany) the Proskomedia is performed during the singing of "Lord, I have cried" while the deacon and concelebrants vest, allowing the extensive Old Testament readings to serve their ancient baptismal catechetical purpose.
[^348]: The Doxastikon of Holy Saturday (*Дне́шнїй де́нь та́йнѡ*): Attributed to the Studite or early Byzantine hymnographers, this hymn parallels Genesis 2:2-3 with Christ's Sabbath rest in the tomb, concluding with His return through the Resurrection to life and eternal glory.
[^349]: Sacerdotal Entrance with the Holy Gospel: Because the Liturgy of St. Basil the Great follows immediately after the Old Testament readings, the Little Entrance of the Liturgy is combined with the Vespers entrance, made with the Gospel Book rather than the censer.
[^350]: The Fifteen Old Testament Lections of Holy Saturday: The ancient Byzantine and Jerusalem cathedral rite appointed these extended readings during the paschal baptismal vigil, while the catechumens were being baptized, chrismated, and led in procession into the temple for the Paschal Eucharist.
[^351]: Typology of the Paschal Lamb: Exodus 12:46 prescribes that no bone of the Paschal lamb shall be broken, fulfilled at Christ's Crucifixion (John 19:36), establishing the central Paschal typology of Christ as the true Lamb of God.
[^352]: The Sign of the Prophet Jonah: Chanted in its entirety across chapters 1 through 4, the Book of Jonah serves as the preeminent Scriptural type of Christ's three-day burial and descent into hades, as declared by Christ Himself in Matthew 12:40.
[^353]: The Cessation of Manna and the Archistrategos: Joshua 5 prefigures the cessation of the Old Testament types (the manna) upon entering the Promised Land and eating the unleavened bread, followed by the Christophany of the Commander (Archistrategos) of the Host of the Lord, venerated by Joshua as holy ground (parallel to Exodus 3:5).
[^354]: The Canticle of Moses at the Red Sea (Exodus 15:1-19): Chanted antiphonally with the refrains "Let us sing unto the Lord: for He hath been gloriously glorified!" following the Sixth Reading, standing as the universal Christian canticle of baptismal illumination, resurrection victory over hades, and deliverance from spiritual Pharaoh.
[^355]: The Prophecy of Zephaniah on the Day of the Resurrection: In Zephaniah 3:8, the LXX text explicitly reads: *ὑπόμεινόν με, λέγει Κύριος, εἰς ἡμέραν ἀναστάσεώς μου εἰς μαρτύριον* ("Wait for Me, saith the Lord, unto the day of My resurrection as a witness"), celebrated as a direct prophetic witness of Christ's resurrection.
[^356]: Elijah, the Widow of Zarephath, and the Inexhaustible Cruse: The miracle of the jar of flour and the cruse of oil that did not fail, followed by the resurrection of the widow's son by Elijah breathing threefold upon him, serves as an Old Testament type of both the inexhaustible Eucharistic gifts and the universal resurrection from the dead.
[^357]: The Robe of Salvation and the Nuptial Joy of the Church: Isaiah 61:10–62:5 provides the baptismal vesting verse ("My soul shall rejoice in the Lord: for He hath clothed me with the robe of salvation...") and celebrates the mystical marriage of the Church, redeemed and adorned for her Bridegroom.
[^358]: The Binding of Isaac (the Akedah): Genesis 22:1–15 stands as the supreme Old Testament sacrifice prefiguring the Father offering His Only-begotten Son on the Cross, carrying the wood of the sacrifice to Mount Moriah (Golgotha), and the ram caught in the thicket by its horns sparing Isaac.
[^359]: The Eleventh Reading: from the Prophecy of Isaiah (Isaiah 61:1-9) — The Messianic Anointing of the Servant of the Lord. Proclaimed by Christ Himself in the synagogue of Nazareth (Luke 4:18-19) as fulfilled in His person, this lection heralds the Paschal transformation from mourning to jubilation ("glory instead of ashes, the anointing of joy to the mourners") and the restoration of the everlasting covenant through the holy priesthood of the new dispensation.
[^360]: The Twelfth Reading: from the Fourth Book of Kings (4 Kings 4:8-37 [2 Kings 4:8-37]) — Elisha and the Resurrection of the Shunammite's Son. The miraculous conception granted to the barren woman, followed by the bodily resurrection of her deceased child through the prophet stretching himself upon him and breathing seven times upon him, prefigures the descent of Christ into hades and His vivification of fallen Adam and all mortal flesh.
[^361]: The Thirteenth Reading: from the Prophecy of Isaiah (Isaiah 63:11–64:5) — The Prophetic Invocation of the Divine Arm and the Rendition of the Heavens. The prophet recalls the exodus of Israel through the Red Sea by the right hand of Moses, invoking the Fatherhood of God ("Thou, O Lord, art our Father, our Redeemer: from the beginning Thy name is upon us") and imploring Him to rend the heavens and descend with consuming fire upon the adversaries.
[^362]: The Fourteenth Reading: from the Prophecy of Jeremiah (Jeremiah 31:31-34) — The New Covenant Written upon the Heart. Foretelling the transition from the external Sinai covenant to the internal sacramental economy of grace, the Lord promises to write His divine laws directly upon the hearts and minds of His people, culminating in the remission of all transgressions through Christ's voluntary sacrifice.
[^363]: The Fifteenth Reading: from the Prophecy of Daniel (Daniel 3:1-56/88) — The Three Youths in the Babylonian Furnace. Chanted at the conclusion of the baptismal lections, the preservation of Ananias, Azarias, and Misael unburned amidst the raging flames by the Angel of the Lord constitutes the supreme Old Testament type of Christ's descent into hades, descending into the fiery realm of death without suffering corruption, while delivering the righteous from eternal fire.
[^364]: The Canticle of the Three Youths (*Бл҃гослови́те всѧ̑ дѣла̀ Гдⷭ҇нѧ* / Daniel 3:57-88): The ancient *Benedicite*, chanted antiphonally between reader and choir while all stand ("We stand here, and sing"), uniting all orders of the cosmos—angels, heavens, waters, celestial luminaries, elements, and the spirits of the just—in the cosmic doxology anticipating the Paschal dawn.
[^365]: The Baptismal Anthem (*Е҆ли́цы во Хрⷭ҇та̀ крести́стесѧ* / Galatians 3:27): Substituted for the Trisagion on Holy Saturday, recalling the historical celebration of the Paschal baptism of the catechumens during the 15 Old Testament readings, after which the newly illumined were led in procession into the temple clad in white robes for the Eucharistic Liturgy.
[^366]: The Epistle of Holy Saturday (Romans 6:3-11, pericope 91): St. Paul's classic theological locus on Holy Baptism as sacramental co-burial and co-resurrection with Christ, asserting that as Christ died unto sin once and lives unto God, the baptized are dead unto sin and alive unto God in Christ Jesus.
[^367]: The Prokeimenon Anthem of Tone 7 (*Воскреснѝ Бж҃е, сꙋдѝ землѝ* / Psalm 81:8 [82:8]): Replacing the customary Alleluia, this triumphant refrain with the verses of Psalm 81 is sung while the sacred ministers divest themselves of their dark Lenten vestments in the sanctuary and vest in white and radiant robes, while the dark paraments of the holy table and church are replaced with bright white coverings.
[^368]: The Gospel of the Resurrection (Matthew 28:1-20, pericope 115): The first liturgical reading of the Resurrection of Christ, narrating the dawn at the sepulchre, the earthquake, the descent of the Angel rolling back the stone, the fear of the guards, the appearance of the Risen Christ to the women, and the universal apostolic commission on the mountain in Galilee.
[^369]: The Cherubic Hymn of Holy Saturday (*Да молчи́тъ вся́каѧ пло́ть человѣ́ча* / Tone 8): Borrowed from the ancient Jerusalem Liturgy of Saint James, this troparion replaces the standard Byzantine *Cherubikon* (*И҆́же херꙋві́мы*), bidding all earthly creation to be silent as the King of kings and Lord of lords comes forth to be slain and given as spiritual food to the faithful, preceded by the celestial hosts.
[^370]: The Blessing of Bread and Wine at the Analoy: Because the Liturgy concluded late in the evening and the faithful remained in church for the vigil leading to midnight Paschal Matins, bread, wine, dates, and figs were blessed upon an analoy in the nave to sustain the worshippers during the reading of the Acts of the Apostles.
[^371]: Reader's Service Rubric for Vespers: When the Divine Liturgy cannot be celebrated due to dire necessity, the Typikon directs the office to conclude as standard Vespers with the Resurrection Aposticha Stichera in Tone 1 from the Oktoechos, preserving the complete hymnographic witness of the Resurrection.
[^372]: The Nocturnal Vigil and the Reading of the Acts of the Apostles: The Typikon specifies that the Liturgy must conclude at the second hour of the night (approx. 8:00 PM). The Slavonic rubric notes the Greek practice where the brethren remain continuously in church, partaking of the blessed bread and wine while the reader begins the continuous reading of the Acts of the Apostles (*Дѣя́нїа ст҃ы́хъ а҆пⷭ҇лъ*).
[^373]: The Canon of Holy Saturday Compline by Symeon Metaphrastes (the Logothete): Entitled "On the Bodily Burial of the Lord God and our Savior Jesus Christ, and on the Lamentation of the Most Holy Theotokos" in Tone 6. The rubric notes that this office and canon are absent from the Greek Triodion, which omits Compline on Holy Saturday evening, witnessing to a distinct Slavic liturgical development.
[^374]: The Lamentation of the Theotokos at the Burial of Christ: Sharing its Heirmoi with the Great Saturday Matins Canon ("He Who in ancient times concealed..."), the troparia of this canon present the maternal grief of the Virgin Mary standing before the Cross, pleading with Joseph of Arimathea to approach Pilate and bury the Body of her Son.
[^375]: Ode 6 Heirmos of Holy Saturday Compline: "Jonah was caught, but not held fast..." In this heirmos, the Prophet Jonah's three-day deliverance from the belly of the sea monster prefigures Christ's bodily rest in the tomb and His triumph over death. The concluding phrase quotes Jonah 2:9 (LXX: "They that observe vanities and lies have forsaken their own mercy"), addressed here directly to the Roman guard ("custodia") keeping watch over Christ's sepulchre.
[^376]: The Holy Saturday Kontakion and Ikos in Tone 6: "He Who shut the abyss is beheld dead..." Traditionally ascribed to Saint Romanos the Melodist, this hymn contemplates the supreme theological antinomy of the Creator resting bodily in the tomb while hades groans below and the Jews plot to slander His Resurrection. The refrain proclaims Holy Saturday as the "most blessed Sabbath" (prefigured in Genesis 2:2-3), wherein Christ rests from all His works of recreation.
[^377]: The Continuous Reading of the Acts of the Apostles: Preserving the ancient Jerusalem nocturnal vigil before Paschal Matins, the Typikon directs that after Compline the reader immediately begins reading the Acts of the Apostles, the biblical account of the Resurrection proclaimed to the nations, continuing until the fourth hour of the night (approx. 10:00 PM) when the bells are struck for the Paschal Midnight Office.
[^378]: The Paschal Midnight Office (Nocturns): Celebrated late on Saturday night before the midnight Paschal vigil, the Midnight Office comprises the repetition of the Great Saturday Matins Canon ("He Who in ancient times concealed the pursuing tyrant...", Tone 6). During Ode 9, the clergy transfer the Holy Shroud (Plashchanytsia) from the tomb in the center of the nave to the holy table in the sanctuary, where it remains for forty days until the Apodosis of Pascha.
[^379]: The Paschal Patristic Homily of Saint Epiphanius of Cyprus: Entitled *In sancto et magno Sabbato* (PG 43:439-464), this classic patristic lection opens with the renowned words: "What is this? Today there is a great silence upon the earth, a great silence and stillness, for the King sleeps." It portrays Christ descending into hades to awaken Adam and Eve and all the righteous of the Old Covenant.
[^380]: The Extended Litany at the Midnight Office: Includes seasonal agricultural petitions according to weather conditions (supplications for rain during drought or for temperate dry weather during unseasonable rains), reflecting the ancient agrarian liturgical cycle embedded in the Slavic Trebnik and Typikon traditions.
[^381]: The Concluding Dismissal of the Holy Saturday Midnight Office: The final liturgical dismissal of the Lenten Triodion period, commemorating Christ Who "voluntarily was laid in the grave in the Flesh and trod down hades." Immediately following this dismissal, the church lights are extinguished in anticipation of the Paschal midnight procession.
[^382]: The Frontispiece Engraving of the Resurrection of Christ (p206): Designed and executed after a sketch under the direction of Evlogy (E. V.) Voronets in 1901 for the Stauropegion typography, this full-page plate depicts the triumphant Risen Christ ascending above the open tomb surrounded by radiant beams of light and angelic choirs, with the biblical caption from Luke 24:34: "Truly the Lord is risen..."
[^383]: The Master Inscription of the Pentecostarion: "Beginning with God the Holy. The Pentecostarion, that is, the Festal Fifty Days..." This formal incipit marks the boundary between the Lenten Triodion (which concluded with the Midnight Office) and the Pentecostarion proper, which encompasses the liturgical period from the dawn of Holy Pascha through the Sunday of All Saints.
[^384]: The Paschal Midnight Procession and Outdoor Matins Opening: At midnight the church bells peal, the temple is filled with incense, and the clergy and faithful process out of the dark sanctuary through the northern doors into the narthex (or outside around the temple) with tapers, icons, and the Holy Gospel, while chanting the Sticheron in Tone 6 ("Thy Resurrection, O Christ Savior..."). The western doors are shut to symbolize the sealed sepulchre of Christ.
[^385]: The Paschal Troparion in Tone 5: "Christ is risen from the dead, trampling down death by death, and upon those in the tombs bestowing life." Chanted for the first time before the closed doors of the temple, this core hymnographic proclamation is repeated three times by the superior and three times by the choir, inaugurating the bright Paschal celebration.
[^386]: The Paschal Verses: Drawn from Psalm 67:1-3 [68:1-3] ("Let God arise, and let His enemies be scattered...") and Psalm 117:24 [118:24] ("This is the day which the Lord hath made..."), these verses are chanted antiphonally between the superior and the choir before the temple doors are struck with the Cross and flung open, symbolizing Christ shattering the gates of brass and breaking the bars of iron in hades.
[^387]: The Paschal Canon of Saint John of Damascus in Tone 1: Known as the "Golden Canon" or the "Queen of Canons," this hymnographic masterpiece of the 8th century is celebrated with dynamic censing of the whole temple and mutual Paschal greetings ("Christ is risen! Truly He is risen!") at every ode, accompanied by the repeated refrain "Christ is risen from the dead."
[^388]: The Paschal Hypakoe in Tone 4: "When they that were with Mary came before the dawn..." Commemorating the holy Myrrh-Bearing Women arriving at the sepulchre before the dawn, where the angel announces to them that Christ is risen and bids them proclaim the glad tidings to the Apostles.
[^389]: The Paschal Reading from Saint Gregory the Theologian: Oration 45 (The Second Paschal Oration, PG 36:623-664), opening with the citation of Habakkuk 2:1: "Upon my watch will I stand, and sit upon the rock..." The prophet Habakkuk stands upon his watch, beholding the light-bearing angel proclaiming the Resurrection of Christ.
[^390]: The Paschal Kontakion in Tone 8: "Though Thou didst descend into the grave, O Immortal One..." Ascribed to Saint Romanos the Melodist, this hymn crystallizes the dogma of the Resurrection: Christ's bodily descent into the grave destroyed the dominion of hades, while His rising brought the apostolic greeting of joy ("Rejoice!") to the Myrrh-Bearers and divine peace to the disciples.
[^391]: The Paschal Synaxarion for the Holy and Great Sunday of Pascha, traditionally attributed to Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos (c. 1256–1335). It expounds the Hebrew etymology of "Pascha" as "passage" (transitus / διαβατήρια), harmonizes the four resurrectional Gospel accounts of the appearances to the Myrrh-Bearing Women and Apostles, and presents two distinct liturgical computations for Christ's "three days in the tomb" (the triduum) based on the Hebrew reckoning of a day-and-night (nychthemeron).
[^392]: The ancient resurrectional hymn *Resurrectionem Christi cernentes* (Воскресе́нїе Хрⷭ҇то́во ви́дѣвше). Unlike throughout the rest of the liturgical year where it is chanted once during Sunday Matins, during the Paschal season it is chanted three times in succession at Matins, at the Paschal Hours, and at the Paschal Typika.
[^393]: Ode 7 Heirmos of the Paschal Canon: "He Who delivered the youths from the furnace..." (Ѻ҆́троки ѿ пе́щи И҆зба́вивый). Based on the Song of the Three Holy Children in the Babylonian furnace (Daniel 3), this hymn contemplates the supreme mystery of the Incarnation: Christ, clothed in our human nature, voluntarily undergoes mortal suffering in order to vest our mortality with the beauty of incorruption.
[^394]: Ode 8 Heirmos of the Paschal Canon: "This chosen and holy day..." (Се́й нарече́нный и҆ ст҃ы́й де́нь). Echoing Gregory the Theologian's Paschal Oration, this hymn proclaims the Day of the Resurrection as the "one of the Sabbaths" (mia ton sabbaton), the King and Lord of days, the "Feast of feasts and Triumph of triumphs."
[^395]: The Paschal Ode 9 Megalynarion: "The angel cried aloud unto Her that is full of grace..." (А҆́ггелъ вопїѧ́ше Бл҃года́тнѣй). This renowned ninth-ode refrain combines the Archangel Gabriel's greeting to the Mother of God with the joyful announcement of the third-day Resurrection of Her Son, bidding the human race to rejoice in the destruction of death.
[^396]: Ode 9 Heirmos of the Paschal Canon: "Shine, shine, O New Jerusalem..." (Свѣти́сѧ свѣти́сѧ но́вый І҆ерꙋсали́ме). Drawing upon Isaiah 60:1 ("Arise, shine, for thy light is come, and the glory of the Lord is risen upon thee") and Revelation 21:2, this triumphant hymn celebrates the Church as the New Jerusalem and calls upon the Mother of God to exult in the bodily rising of Her Son.
[^397]: The Paschal Exapostilarion *Carne cum obdormivisses* (Пло́тїю оу҆сну́въ ꙗ҆́кѡ ме́ртвъ), an Automelon (having its own unique melody, traditionally assigned to Tone 3). It employs the poetic scriptural idiom of sleep for Christ's voluntary bodily death in the sepulchre, through which the forefather Adam is raised and mortality abolished.
[^398]: The Paschal Stichera in Tone 5: "A sacred Pascha is revealed unto us today..." (Па́сха свѧще́ннаѧ на́мъ дне́сь показа́ся). Traditionally ascribed to Saint John of Damascus, these festive troparia are intercalated with the prophetic verses of Psalm 67 [68], concluding with the majestic Paschal Doxasticon ("It is the day of Resurrection...") commanding universal reconciliation and mutual forgiveness in the light of Christ's victory.
[^399]: The Paschal Kiss of Peace and Rite of Mutual Forgiveness (Цѣлова́нїе). Standing before the holy doors, the superior, clergy, monastic brethren, workmen, and laity exchange the joyful Paschal greeting ("Christ is risen! Truly He is risen!"), venerating the precious Cross, the Holy Gospel, and the icons, embodying in liturgical action the spiritual pardon proclaimed in the Doxasticon: "Let us forgive all things by the Resurrection."
[^400]: The Paschal Catechetical Oration of Saint John Chrysostom (*Sermo Catecheticus in Pascha*, PG 59:721-724). Universally appointed to be read at Paschal Matins while the entire congregation stands holding candles, this homily invites all believers without exception—whether they fasted or neglected the fast, whether they labored from the first, third, sixth, ninth, or eleventh hour—to partake of the rich banquet of faith, culminating in the triumphant Pauline cry: "O death, where is thy sting? O hades, where is thy victory?" (1 Cor. 15:55).
[^401]: The Troparion of Saint John Chrysostom in Tone 8: "The grace shining forth from thy mouth like a torch..." Chanted immediately following the reading of his Paschal Catechetical Oration, this hymn expresses filial gratitude to the Golden-mouthed Archbishop of Constantinople for his theological illumination of the universe.
[^402]: The Order of the Paschal Hours (Часы̀ Па́схи). Throughout Bright Week, the entire ordinary Psalmody of the Daily Office (the First, Third, Sixth, and Ninth Hours, as well as Compline and the Midnight Office) is replaced by this concise, joyful sequence composed of the Paschal Troparion, the Hypakoe, the Kontakion, and resurrectional troparia, omitting all penitential prayers and prostrations.
[^403]: The Order of Typika (Изобразительны / Obednitsa) during Bright Week. Appointed to be read in place of the Divine Liturgy in the absence of a priest, this rite replaces the typical psalms with the Paschal Troparion and integrates the Beatitudes taken from Odes 3 and 6 of the Paschal Canon, together with the Epistle and Gospel pericopes of the day.
[^404]: The Order of the Paschal Moleben (Supplicatory Canon). Celebrated during Paschaltide, this festive service incorporates the Paschal Canon, the Paschal Stichera, the resurrectional Prokeimenon, and the reading of the appearance of the Risen Christ on the road to Emmaus (Luke 24:12-35, pericope 113), concluding with the blessing of the faithful with the precious Cross.
[^405]: The Opening of the Paschal Divine Liturgy. Celebrated early in the morning following Matins and the Paschal Hours, the Liturgy begins with the solemn triadic exclamation ("Blessed is the Kingdom..."), followed by the thrice-repeated chanting of the Paschal Troparion by the clergy within the sanctuary and by the choirs in the nave, inaugurating the Eucharistic celebration of Bright Week.
[^406]: The Three Paschal Antiphons. Composed of selected verses from Psalms 65, 66, and 67 [LXX 65, 66, 67 / MT 66, 67, 68] with the refrains "Through the prayers of the Mother of God, O Savior, save us" (Antiphon 1) and "Save us, O Son of God, Who art risen from the dead..." (Antiphon 2), followed by the Hymn of the Incarnation ("Only-begotten Son and Word of God...").
[^407]: The Paschal Eisodikon (Introit) and Baptismal Hymn. The Little Entrance is made with the verse: "In the assemblies bless ye God, the Lord from the fountains of Israel" (Ps. 67:27 [68:26]). In place of the Trisagion, the ancient baptismal proclamation from Galatians 3:27 is chanted: "As many of you as have been baptized into Christ have put on Christ. Alleluia" (Є҆ли́цы во Хрⷭ҇та̀ крⷭ҇ти́стеся...), testifying to the early Christian practice of administering baptism to catechumens during the Paschal Vigil.
[^408]: The Opening Pericope of the Acts of the Apostles (Acts 1:1-8, pericope 1). The liturgical reading of the Acts of the Apostles begins on the Holy and Great Sunday of Pascha and continues daily throughout the fifty days of the Pentecostarion, proclaiming the witness of the Holy Apostles to the Resurrection of Jesus Christ and the descent of the Holy Spirit.
[^409]: The solemn Paschal Gospel reading ceremony (John 1:1-17, pericope 1). In Slavic and Byzantine liturgical practice, the Prologue of Saint John is proclaimed with exceptional solemnity: concelebrating priests and deacons are stationed at various intervals along the nave from the holy table to the western doors, reading the text responsively in sections (*statiai*), often in multiple languages (such as Greek, Latin, Hebrew, and vernacular tongues), symbolizing the preaching of the Gospel unto all ends of the earth. At each verse or section, the church bells and semantra (*bilo*) are rung, culminating in a triumphant full peal at the conclusion of the reading.
[^410]: The continuous unclosing of the sanctuary doors throughout Bright Week. In Byzantine and Slavic liturgical tradition, the Royal Doors (the Great Gates) and the deacon's doors of the iconostasis remain wide open day and night continuously from the Paschal Midnight Office through Bright Saturday, even during the clergy communion, signifying the torn veil of the Temple (Matt. 27:51), the shattered gates of hades, the open tomb of Christ, and the unimpeded access of all believers into the heavenly Holy of Holies through the Resurrection.
[^411]: The blessing of Paschal foods (flesh-meats, cheese, and eggs) on the Holy and Great Sunday of Pascha. Concluding the strict fasting of Great Lent and Holy Week, this customary benediction sanctifies the festive foods brought by the faithful, drawing typological parallels from Old Testament sacrifices (Abraham's ram, Abel's lamb) and the fatted calf slain for the return of the prodigal son (Luke 15:23).
[^412]: Canonical prohibition against bringing flesh-meats into the sanctuary or church temple. The rubrist provides an extensive dogmatic and canonical instruction distinguishing between blessed festive food and the true Eucharistic Pascha—Christ Himself, the Lamb of God (1 Cor. 5:7). Citing Canon 3 of the Holy Apostles (forbidding the offering of milk, honey, meats, or fermented beverages at the holy table) and Canon 94 of the Quinisext Council in Trullo (Canon 99 in standard Greek collections, condemning the Armenian custom of preparing and eating meats within sacred precincts), the rubric strictly mandates that the blessing of foods must take place outside the temple or in the narthex.
[^413]: The Order of the Artos (Послѣ́дованїе А҆́ртоса). The Artos (Greek ἄρτος, "leavened bread") is a large loaf consecrated at the end of the Paschal Liturgy bearing the icon of the Resurrection or the sign of the Cross, symbolizing Christ as the living Bread of Life (John 6:35, 48) Who visibly remained with His disciples after His rising. In monastic typika, the Artos is carried in solemn procession to the refectory (*trapeza*) with singing and bell-ringing, elevated in place of the Panagia after the meal with the joyful exclamation "Christ is risen!", venerated by all the brethren, and returned to the church, where it rests before the iconostasis until its ceremonial distribution on Bright Saturday.
[^414]: The Paschal Order for the Burial of the Dead during Bright Week (Послѣ́дованїе погребе́нїѧ въ свѣ́тлꙋю Седми́цꙋ). If a Christian falls asleep in the Lord during Bright Week, the usual mournful funeral rites, penitential psalms, and dirges of the Trebnik are replaced by joyful Paschal hymns, the Paschal Canon, and the triumphal proclamation of Christ's victory over hades, testifying that for the faithful, death has been transformed into a Paschal passage (*transitus*) unto eternal life.
[^415]: The Paschal Agape Vespers (Вече́рнѧ любвѝ / *Vesperae Charitatis*). Celebrated on the afternoon of Pascha Sunday, the clergy vest in full festive priestly vestments. The service begins solemnly with the Paschal verses before the holy table, the Little Entrance is made with the Holy Gospel Book, and the Gospel of the Resurrection (John 20:19-25) is read facing the people, frequently in multiple languages.
[^416]: The Tone 2 Octoechos resurrectional stichera appointed for Paschal Agape Vespers. Although the Paschal Canon is chanted in Tone 1, the Octoechos cycle resumes at Paschal Vespers, beginning with Tone 2, with each subsequent day of Bright Week advancing through the tones of the Octoechos (Bright Monday: Tone 2; Bright Tuesday: Tone 3; Bright Wednesday: Tone 4; Bright Thursday: Tone 5; Bright Friday: Tone 6; Bright Saturday: Tone 8).
[^417]: The Anatolian (Eastern / Ἀνατολικά) Stichera. In the Byzantine Octoechos, these resurrectional hymns are traditionally attributed to Patriarch Anatolius of Constantinople (reigned 449–458), who composed four additional stichera for each tone to accompany the psalms at Vespers and Praises.
[^418]: The Dogmatic Theotokion (*Dogmatikon*) of Tone 2: "The shadow of the law passed away..." (Пре́йде сѣ́нь зако́ннаѧ). Attributed to Saint John of Damascus, this hymn juxtaposes the Old Testament typologies of the unburnt bush of Horeb (Exodus 3:2) and the pillar of fire (Exodus 13:21) with the mystery of the Virgin Birth and the Incarnation of Christ, the Sun of Righteousness.
[^419]: The Solemn Entrance with the Gospel at Agape Vespers. Unlike ordinary daily or Sunday Vespers where the Little Entrance is made with the censer alone, at Paschal Vespers the deacon and priest make the entrance carrying the precious Gospel Book, in anticipation of the solemn proclamation of the Resurrection Gospel.
[^420]: The Great Prokeimenon of Paschal Vespers, Tone 7: "What God is so great as our God? Thou art the God Who workest wonders!" (Ps. 76:14-15a [77:13-14a]). Chanted in the festive Grave Tone (Tone 7) with elaborate melismatic melodies, this ancient responsory is repeated on Bright Saturday evening and at Great Feasts of the Lord.
[^421]: The Agape Vespers Gospel reading: John 20:19-25 (pericope 65). Recounting the appearance of the Risen Lord Jesus to the disciples on the evening of the first day of the week, the shut doors, the greeting of peace, the exhibition of His wounds, the insufflation of the Holy Spirit with the authority to remit and retain sins, and the absence of the Apostle Thomas.
[^422]: Bright Monday (Понедѣ́льникъ свѣ́тлыѧ Седми́цы). The second day of Paschal celebration, commemorating the continuing radiance of Christ's Resurrection. In monastic usage, the priest begins Matins vested simply in mantle and epitrachelion, whereas in parochial usage, full vestments are worn throughout the week.
[^423]: Continuous Patristic Reading during Paschaltide. The rubric specifies that beginning on Bright Monday, the monastic community begins the reading of Saint John Chrysostom's *Homilies on the Gospel of Saint John* (PG 59), reading sequentially through this monumental exegetical commentary during Matins throughout the fifty days until the Sunday of All Saints.
[^424]: The Bright Monday Cross Procession (Хре́стний хід). Following Matins and the First Hour, a solemn procession is made around the monastery grounds or church precincts bearing banners, the Gospel, the Cross, the icon of the Resurrection, and the Artos, singing the Paschal Canon and reading the four Resurrection Gospels at the four sides of the church.
[^425]: The Epistle reading for Bright Monday: Acts 1:12-17, 21-26 (pericope 2). Chronicling the return of the Apostles to the upper room in Jerusalem after the Ascension and the choosing of Matthias by lot to fill the apostolic ministry vacated by Judas.
[^426]: The Gospel reading for Bright Monday: John 1:18-28 (pericope 2). The second section of the Johannine Prologue, witnessing that the Only-Begotten Son has declared the invisible God, and recording Saint John the Baptist's testimony unto the Priests and Levites: "I baptize with water, but there stands one among you whom ye know not."
[^427]: Bright Monday Vespers (for Bright Tuesday). In accordance with the Bright Week progression, the stichera on "Lord, I have cried" shift from Tone 2 to Tone 3 of the Octoechos, celebrating Christ's victory over the grave in the third mode.
[^428]: The Dogmatic Theotokion of Tone 3: "How can we not marvel at Thy divine-human childbearing..." (Ка́кѡ не диви́мсѧ Бг҃омꙋ́жномꙋ Рождествꙋ̀ твоему̀). Composed by Saint John of Damascus, this dogmatic hymn expounds the Chalcedonian confession of the two unconfused natures in the single Person of Christ, born of the Father without mother before all ages, and born in the flesh without father from the Virgin Mother of God in the fullness of time.
[^429]: The Great Prokeimenon of Bright Monday Vespers, Tone 7: "Our God is in heaven and on earth: whatsoever He hath willed, He hath done!" (Ps. 113:11 [115:3]). Appointed with verses taken from the ancient Passover Hallel (Psalm 113 [114/115]), proclaiming the exodus of Israel from Egypt and the Jordan turning back as prophetic types of Christ's deliverance of humanity from hades.
[^430]: Bright Tuesday Matins (Во Вто́рникъ свѣ́тлыѧ Седми́цы на Оу҆́трени). The third day of Bright Week, which pairs the Paschal Canon with the resurrectional stichera of Tone 3 at the Praises, celebrating the women bearing myrrh and the confused flight of the Roman sepulchre guard.
[^431]: The Epistle reading for Bright Tuesday: Acts 2:14-21 (pericope 4). Proclaiming Saint Peter's inaugural sermon on the day of Pentecost, applying the prophecy of the Prophet Joel (Joel 2:28-32 [3:1-5]) concerning the outpouring of the Holy Spirit upon all flesh in the last days, the celestial wonders and signs on earth, and the salvation of whosoever shall call upon the Name of the Lord.
[^432]: The continuous Paschal Kinonikon (Communion Hymn): "Receive ye the Body of Christ; taste ye of the immortal Fountain" (Тѣ́ло Хрⷭ҇то́во прїими́те, и҆сто́чника безсме́ртнагѡ вкꙋси́те). Chanted at every Divine Liturgy throughout Bright Week. In addition, this leaf marks an irregularity in the 1907 Lviv printer's folio coordinates: physical leaf p231 bears printed folio скѕ (fol. 226), followed on subsequent leaves by typographical misprints in the Cyrillic folio headings (repeating скз, ски, and сл҃а across fols. 227-234) before stabilizing.
[^433]: Bright Tuesday Vespers (for Bright Wednesday). In accordance with the Bright Week progression through the Octoechos, the liturgical choir advances to Tone 4, chanting the resurrectional stichera and Anatolian hymns attributed to Patriarch Anatolius of Constantinople, praising Christ's harrowing of hades and the smashing of the gates of death.
[^434]: The Dogmatic Theotokion (*Dogmatikon*) of Tone 4: "David the prophet, who through thee became the forefather of God..." (И҆́же тебѐ ра́ди, Бг҃ооте́цъ проро́къ Дв҃дъ). Attributed to Saint John of Damascus, this hymn interprets King David's messianic psalm (Psalm 44:10 [45:9], "The Queen stood at Thy right hand") as a prophetic type of the Virgin Theotokos, and weaves together the parable of the lost sheep (Luke 15:4-7) with the dogma of the Incarnation, wherein the Good Shepherd shoulders fallen humanity and presents it restored unto the Father.
[^435]: Bright Wednesday Matins and Divine Liturgy (Въ Сре́дꙋ свѣ́тлыѧ Седми́цы на Оу҆́трени). Following Matins, monastic typika prescribe a solemn Litiya and procession outside the monastery precincts (*внѣ̀ ѻ҆би́тели*), accompanied by the ringing of bells and the singing of Paschal stichera. At the Liturgy, the Epistle (Acts 2:22-36, pericope 5) continues Saint Peter's proclamation of Christ's resurrection and ascension to the right hand of God, citing Psalm 15 [16] and Psalm 109 [110], while the Gospel (John 1:35-51, pericope 6) records the call of Andrew, Peter, Philip, and Nathanael.
[^436]: Bright Wednesday Vespers (for Bright Thursday). The daily cycle advances to Tone 5 of the Octoechos. The Tone 5 Dogmatic Theotokion, "In the Red Sea there was once depicted an image of the unwedded Bride..." (Въ Чермнѣ́мъ мо́ри, неискꙋсобра́чныѧ Невѣ́сты), provides a typology contrasting Moses parting the Red Sea for the dryshod passage of Israel (Exodus 14) with Gabriel announcing the virginal, seedless conception of Christ the Emmanuel.
[^437]: Bright Thursday Matins and Divine Liturgy (Въ Четверто́къ свѣ́тлыѧ Седми́цы). Matins features Tone 5 resurrectional stichera highlighting the confusion of the guards and the mystery of the sealed tomb. At the Liturgy, the Epistle (Acts 2:38-43, pericope 7) records the baptism of three thousand souls on Pentecost, and the Gospel (John 3:1-15, pericope 8) presents Christ's nocturnal discourse with Nicodemus regarding regeneration of water and the Spirit.
[^438]: Bright Thursday Vespers (for Bright Friday). Advancing to Tone 6 of the Octoechos, the service features the solemn chanting of the Great Prokeimenon in Tone 7: "I will love Thee, O Lord, my strength: the Lord is my foundation and my refuge!" (Ps. 17:2-3a [18:1-2a]), accompanied by three responsory verses from Psalm 17 [18], celebrating God's triumphant deliverance of His servant from death and the nethermost pit.
[^439]: Bright Friday Matins and Divine Liturgy (Въ Пѧто́къ свѣ́тлыѧ Седми́цы). In Byzantine and Slavic tradition, Bright Friday is also widely observed with the commemoration of the Life-Giving Spring (*Живоно́сный Исто́чникъ*) of the Mother of God. The Pentecostarion text appoints the Tone 6 resurrectional stichera at Praises, followed at Liturgy by the Epistle (Acts 3:1-8, pericope 7), describing the miraculous healing of the lame man at the Beautiful Gate of the Temple, and the Gospel (John 2:12-22, pericope 7), recounting the cleansing of the Temple in Jerusalem and Christ's prophecy concerning the temple of His Body.
[^440]: Bright Friday Vespers (for Bright Saturday). The liturgical cycle reaches the final mode of the week, Tone 8 of the Octoechos. The stichera at "Lord, I have cried" celebrate the harrowing of hades, concluding with the Dogmatic Theotokion of Tone 8: "The King of heaven out of love for mankind appeared on earth..." (Цр҃ь нб҃ный за чл҃вѣколю́бїе на землѝ ꙗ҆ви́сѧ), which articulates the Chalcedonian confession of Christ's two natures in one hypostasis without confusion or separation.
[^441]: Bright Saturday Matins (Въ Сꙋббѡ́тꙋ свѣ́тлыѧ Седми́цы на Оу҆́трени). The concluding morning service of Bright Week, bringing the week-long succession through the Octoechos tones to completion in Tone 8. At the conclusion of the Divine Liturgy on this day, the ancient prayer for the breaking and distribution of the Artos (*раздроблѐнїе А҆́ртоса*) is recited, and the Royal Doors of the iconostasis are closed prior to the celebration of the Ninth Hour and Thomas Sunday Vespers.
[^442]: Bright Saturday Epistle and Gospel: Acts 3:11-16 (pericope 8), proclaiming Saint Peter's address in Solomon's Porch after the healing of the lame man, testifying that the God of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob hath glorified His Servant Jesus, Whom the Jews delivered up, but God raised from the dead; and John 3:22-33 (pericope 11), recounting Saint John the Baptist's final testimony unto Christ in Judea ("He that hath the bride is the bridegroom... He must increase, but I must decrease"). In addition, this leaf codifies the monastic typikon for the solemn elevation, distribution, and consumption of the Artos (*раздроблѐнїе А҆́ртоса*) and the elevation of the Panagia bread at the refectory trapeza concluding Bright Week.
